# THE MYSTICAL JANUS <note>original: "Janus Mysticus." Janus was the two-faced Roman god of beginnings, transitions, and time, looking simultaneously into the past and the future. Here, the author uses the image to signify an examination of theology’s history and its current state.</note>
## LOOKING BACK
->or<-
### Scriptural
# **Contemplation**
->of<-
# **MYSTICAL THEOLOGY,** <note>Mystical theology generally refers to the study of direct, subjective experiences of the divine.</note>
->what it<-
## **Originally was,**
## what it later became,
->and<-
## **what is to be thought of it.**
->Presented briefly and clearly<-
->by<-
# **JOHANN FRANK, of Mecklenburg.**

---

->Rostock and Neubrandenburg.<-
### **Published by Georg Ludwig Fritsch.**
### 1723.

# Preface.

When I then considered a title, *Janus* <note>The Roman god of beginnings, gates, and transitions, famously depicted with two faces looking in opposite directions—one to the past and one to the future</note> immediately occurred to me. And this was all the more so because I had directed my thoughts partly toward the past and partly toward the future. Therefore, *Janus* with his two faces seemed so much more appropriate to me. However, this time I will represent him only as looking backward, and consider *Mysticism* <note>original: "Mysticam"; referring here to the history and practice of seeking direct, internal communion with God</note> as it was practiced in past times. What was it then, and what has it become? If God <note>original: "GOtt"</note> gives strength and life, I shall also represent him as looking forward, and show how one might proceed in a

# **JESUS** Lead Me.

## §. I.

**Mystical Theology** <term>Theologia Mystica</term> <note>original: "Theologia Mystica"; a branch of Christian theology focusing on the soul's direct, experiential union with the Divine</note> is intended to mean a secret **Doctrine of God** <term>Gottes-Lehre</term>. It is, however, a subject that lacks for enemies just as little as it lacks for friends. Just as the latter fight for it and wish to exalt it to the heavens, the former seek not only to cast it down, but even to banish it from the Church entirely.

## §. II.

When the **Tarquins** <term>Tarquinii</term> <note>original: "Tarquinii"; referring to the legendary final dynasty of Roman kings who were expelled to establish the Roman Republic</note> in **Rome** behaved poorly, the **Romans** did not just drive them out of the **City**; they also no longer wished to tolerate their **Name** within it. For this reason, in **Roman History**, one will not easily find anyone after that **Time** who was named **Tarquinius**. Some would like to do the same with **Mystical Theology**, doing away with the **Name** along with the **Subject**—

<meta>catchword: do away.</meta>

<summary>The author introduces Mystical Theology as a "secret doctrine of God," noting that it is a deeply polarizing subject that some wish to strike from the Church's records entirely, much like the Romans struck the name of the Tarquin kings from their history.</summary>

<keywords>Mystical Theology, Tarquins, Roman History, church controversy, secret doctrine, religious terminology</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous section, addressing the controversy surrounding the name "Mystical Theology" and calling for a charitable, evidence-based evaluation of the tradition.</meta>

<header>Scriptural Reflection</header>

do. Both of which seem somewhat too heated.

### §. III.

We certainly tolerate the name in other matters, when we deal with the mystical sense of Holy Scripture <note>original: "de sensu S. S. mystico"; S. S. stands for *Sacra Scriptura*</note>, the mystical union <note>original: "de unione mystica"; the spiritual doctrine of the soul's direct experience of God</note>, and so forth. Why not also with *Theology*? The name would likely suffice, if the subject itself is good.

### §. IV.

But, they say, that is where it fails! So it is. Many complaints are brought against it. But would it not be necessary, before one condemns something, to first present its true original nature, and also show what was added later and how it all aligns with Scripture? In this way, the good that may be in it could then be distinguished from the evil that might be present.

### §. V.

Love surely binds us to hope for the best from our neighbor whenever possible, and thus also to think that the dear ancients <note>Referring to the early Church Fathers and desert monastics</note>, from whom *Mystical Theology* originates, had no intention of presenting anything against **God’s** Word. This is because salvation—which cannot be attained without **God’s** Word—was undoubtedly just as dear to them as it is to us. Just as the *Mystics* <term>Mystici</term> <note>original: "Mystici"; practitioners of internal, experiential prayer and contemplation</note> to this very day boast that they [take] **God’s** Word

<meta>catchword: to the</meta>

<summary>The author defends the use of the term "Mystical Theology" by comparing it to other accepted theological concepts and argues that the tradition should be judged by its original intent and scriptural alignment rather than later corruptions.</summary>
<keywords>Mystical Theology, Mystical Union, Scriptural Interpretation, Church Fathers, Charity, Discernment, Mystics, Word of God</keywords>

<header>of Mystical Theology.</header>

as their foundation. Although not everyone does so with equal justification at all times.

->§. VI.<-

And because the *Mystical* <note>original: "Mystica"</note>, according to its name, employs many dark and hidden words, it is only fair that one first strives for a proper understanding, and afterwards holds this against the Holy Scripture to see whether it is scriptural or not.

->§. VII.<-

Whether this has been done thoroughly, those will know who have looked extensively into mystical matters. I have seen no one who has given me complete light. I will therefore—as much as God gives me grace—judge the matters that are common to almost everyone, according to my ability, in love, in such a way that it can happen without violating the honor and doctrine of our God, and indicate what my opinion is. However, I would not want to hinder anyone from putting forward something better and more certain. May God lead me and all people into all truth.

->§. IIX.<-

First, we wish to examine the threefold mystical way <note>The traditional stages of the spiritual life: purification (purgatio), illumination (illuminatio), and union (unio)</note>, which is the oldest, and which the Mystics <term>Mystici</term> <note>Practitioners of internal, experiential spirituality</note> would gladly derive from *Augustine* and *Jerome*. These are approved by the...

### §. IX.

Regarding the first, no particular brooding <note>intensive speculation or over-analysis</note>

their version, but our repentance is grounded in Scripture.
As this has been sufficiently proven by the teachers of
our churches, it does not require my further labor.

<vocab>Weg der Reinigung (Way of Purification), Weg der Erleuchtung (Way of Enlightenment), Weg der Vereinigung (Way of Union), Busse (Repentance/Penance), Päbstischen (Papists/Catholics), Mystischen Lehrer (Mystical Teachers), Zweck (Goal/Purpose)</vocab>

<summary>The author defines the "threefold way" of spiritual life—purification, enlightenment, and union—arguing that the first stage must be understood as scriptural repentance rather than Catholic penance. He also addresses a logical tension in mystical theory: whether "union" is a process (a way) or the final result (a goal).</summary>

<header>of Mystical Theology.</header>

union <note>completing the word "un-ion" from the previous page</note> distinguishes. This is the goal. Thus, the former can certainly be counted among the ways. Although I would rather say that there is only one way, upon which he who wishes to come to union with **God** must take three steps. And so the work of union would be the last step, through which he could come into the state of union.

### § XI.

The middle step, known as the Way of Enlightenment, holds no small difficulty. A correct explanation of it can bring great benefit, but an incorrect one can cause great harm. I do not concern myself with what the modern mystics <note>original Latin: "Mystici"</note> make of it. I wish to see, free from all prejudice, what explanation Holy Scripture allows, as this was undoubtedly the opinion of the ancient mystics <note>original Latin: "Mysticorum"</note>.

### § XII.

Enlightenment is a figurative <note>original: "verblümt," meaning flowery or metaphorical</note> word, and is intended to mean instruction in matters that a person did not previously know. And indeed, in spiritual matters, it can be nothing other than the work of the Holy Spirit, who through the Word and the Sacraments <note>original Latin: "Sacramenta"</note> gives us humans instruction in the things that are necessary for our salvation <term>Seligkeit</term> <note>This term refers to both eternal salvation and the state of spiritual blessedness</note>. It is, however, [added/continued]

<summary>The author clarifies that the "Way of Union" is the final step toward a spiritual state and begins defining "Enlightenment" as the Holy Spirit's instruction through Word and Sacrament.</summary>
<keywords>Way of Union, Way of Enlightenment, Holy Spirit, Sacraments, Salvation, Mystics, Biblical Interpretation</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of "Enlightenment," transitioning from its divine source to its practical application through preachers and its necessity for the spiritual life.</meta>

<header>Scriptural Contemplation</header>

ascribed <note>completing the sentence from the previous page: "It is, however, ascribed..."</note> not only to **God**, as is seen here and there in the Holy Scripture: but also to preachers. Thus **Paul** says: a) that to him was given the grace to enlighten everyone. It deals directly with the understanding or spiritual eyes. This **Paul** summarizes when He wishes, b) that **God** would give his listeners enlightened eyes of their understanding. It aims, however, toward the goal that the will be converted and the desires rightly directed, to conform themselves to the will of **God**, so that His will alone may be done in all things.

### §. XIII.

This instruction and this enlightenment <term>Erleuchtung</term> <note>In this tradition, enlightenment is the second stage of spiritual growth, where the soul receives divine clarity</note> is necessary for us in the whole of Christianity from beginning to end. For just as one who sits in darkness sees or recognizes nothing, so we know nothing of spiritual matters; we also do not know **God** and **Jesus**. But when we can hear or even read **God’s** word, then **God** opens our eyes, so that we see the wonders in **God’s** law. c)

### §. XIV.

Without this enlightenment, even the first way—known as the way of purification <term>Reinigung</term> or repentance <term>Busse</term> <note>The "Via Purgativa," or the initial stage of turning away from sin and cleansing the soul</note>—cannot

repentance cannot exist. Is it not so? Whoever is to be purified must know that he is impure. Whoever does not know—or does not want to know—that he is impure, does not concern himself with any washing. Whoever wants to be purified must also know the means through which one can be purified. He must also know that purification is necessary. We know nothing of any of these things by nature.

<margin>d) Romans 7:7.</margin>
### §. XV.

We can prove this with the example of **Paul**. He was a learned man; yet with all his learning, he could not reach the point of knowing that desire <note>original: "Lust," referring here to concupiscence or the internal inclination toward sin</note> was sin. "I knew nothing of desire," he says, "if the Law had not said: *You shall not covet*." d) In this, we also see at the same time how we should come to the recognition of our impurity. As he also says shortly before: "I did not recognize sin except through the Law." This is therefore the first work of the **Holy Spirit**, that He brings us to the knowledge of sins through the Law. But He also teaches that one must find relief from such filth <note>original: "Unflat," a strong term for moral corruption or spiritual uncleanness</note> and purify oneself, and thus this way of purification <term>Weg der Reinigung</term> <note>The "Via Purgativa," the first phase of the mystical life involving the purging of vices</note> is highly necessary.

<meta>catchword: How</meta>

<summary>The author argues that spiritual purification is impossible without the Holy Spirit's enlightenment, as humans are naturally unaware of their own impurity. Using the Apostle Paul as an example, he explains that only through God's Law can one recognize internal desire as sin and begin the necessary process of cleansing.</summary>

# Scriptural Contemplation

As seen in **Isaiah**, <margin>e) Isaiah 1:16.</margin> where it says: *Wash yourselves, make yourselves clean. Put away your evil nature from before my eyes.* So He also teaches in the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelio"</note> the means by which we can be purified: *The blood of Jesus Christ, the Son of God, makes us clean from all sin.* <margin>f) 1 John 1:7.</margin> For if the blood of oxen and goats, and the ashes of a heifer sprinkled [upon them], sanctifies those who are unclean to a physical purity, how much more will the blood of **Christ**—who offered Himself without any blemish <note>original: "ohne allen Wandel," an archaic expression meaning "without spot" or "unblemished"</note> through the **Holy Spirit** to **God**—purify our conscience from dead works, to serve the living God. <margin>g) Hebrews 9:13.</margin> Here, all human reason must confess that it would know nothing of all these things if the Holy Spirit had not revealed them through the Word.

->### §. XVI.<-

Just as the **Holy Spirit** begins with this instruction regarding purification, so He continues with it until conversion <term>Bekehrung</term> <note>In this context, conversion is the definitive turning of the soul away from the world and toward God, a key milestone in the mystical journey.</note> occurs, or until the person pushes this benefit away from themselves. We have clear examples of this, both of those who used this grace rightly, and those who misused it. In **Acts 2**, **Peter** delivers to the Jews a ser-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Holy Spirit's role in conversion, picking up from Peter’s sermon in Acts 2. It compares different reactions to the Word of God, specifically looking at how biblical figures like Festus, Felix, and Agrippa responded to Paul’s message.</meta>

preached; Acts 24. Paul to **Festus**, and chapter 26 to **Felix** and **Agrippa**. But with unequal results. To all of these, the Word is preached. They heard it as well. They also held a concept of it in their understanding. Their will and the emotions of the soul <note>original: "Gemüths-Bewegungen," referring to the internal stirrings or passions of the mind and heart.</note> were also stirred by it. Upon this, conversion should have followed. This indeed happened among many Jews. But many Jews (Acts 2), as also **Festus**, **Felix**, and **Agrippa** did, when they had come so far, [closed] their eyes and their heart, and would not look upon the light any further.

### §. XVII.

Meanwhile, the **Holy Spirit** has done His work in both parties. For the knowledge and the movement found on both sides is nothing other than a work of the **Holy Spirit**, and thus has been a supernatural, Divine work. The originator <term>Uhrheber</term> <note>An archaic spelling of "Urheber," meaning the primary source or author of an action.</note> and the principal working cause is one and the same: namely, the **Holy Spirit**. The means is the same: namely, the preaching of the **Divine Word**. Also, the aim is the same: namely, the listeners' conversion and salvation <term>Seligkeit</term> <note>This term refers to the state of eternal happiness and blessedness in God's presence.</note>. However, that some receive the grace, while others [push] it from themselves—

The physician offers his medicine <term>Artzeney</term> from a glass: to one who accepts it and uses it rightly, and to another who pushes it away from himself, or hinders its effect through a wicked way of life. Should the rejected medicine, for that reason, lose its worthiness and internal excellence? Whoever would say that the knowledge <term>Erkäntniß</term> attained by *Festus*, *Felix*, and *Agrippa* was a natural, fleshly, or devilish science <note>original: "Wissenschafft." In this period, the term referred broadly to a systematic body of knowledge or understanding rather than modern laboratory science.</note>, cannot be seen as anything other than an insulter of the great God. It cannot be called natural or fleshly: for here too, what *Christ* says holds true: **Flesh and blood has not revealed this to you.** h) For what does a person know of and by himself concerning *Christ* and His benefits? It cannot be called devilish either, because it does not have its origin <term>Uhrsprung</term> <note>An archaic spelling of "Ursprung," meaning source or origin.</note> from the Devil, but from God.

### §. XX.

Since this is now indisputably the case, why is it that the <term>Mystics</term> <note>original: "Mystici." The author refers to the tradition of mystical theology—associated with figures like Pseudo-Dionysius—which outlines the soul's journey to God.</note> have restricted illumination <term>Erleuchtung</term> so narrowly, and placed it as if imprisoned between purification <term>Reinigung</term> and union <term>Vereinigung</term>? <note>The author is discussing the "Threefold Way" (the *triplex via*), a classic spiritual framework consisting of three stages: the Purgative (cleansing from sin), the Illuminative (enlightenment of the mind), and the Unitive (union with God). This is a common practice in all languages—to name the whole while meaning only a part. Those who know what a <term>Synecdoche</term> <note>A figure of speech in which a part is used to represent the whole (e.g., saying "hired hands" to mean workers).</note> is in the art of rhetoric <term>Redner-Kunst</term> will readily admit this. Otherwise, we would have to assume that the ancient <term>Mystics</term> were reckless people who thoughtlessly babbled whatever came to mind and maintained no scriptural order in their presentation. This is seen in the words of Christ when he says: "He who abides in me and I in him, he brings forth much fruit: for without me you can do nothing." q) Therefore, renewal cannot be understood as being the same thing as illumination.

### §. XXVI.

In the meantime, we must observe concerning this threefold mystical path that while the first and last steps are presented clearly enough, the middle one has been shrouded in a mystical cloak <term>Mäntelchen</term> <note>Literally "little cloak," this suggests that the middle stage of the spiritual journey—Illumination—is often described by mystical writers in intentionally obscure or "hidden" language.</note>. Because this was not done without purpose, everyone is given reason to ponder the cause.

### §. XXVII.

Meanwhile, let us proceed and look at other matters. Here we immediately encounter what others—those who do not walk the threefold path—consider necessary to reach union with **GOD**, as their established goal. In this, one finds various things.

### §. XXIIX.

Firstly, one finds there reading, reflecting or meditating, and beholding or contemplating. If we are to judge these according to **Scripture**, the first two can be understood as nothing other than the proper use of the Means of Grace <term>Gnaden-Mittel</term> <note>In Lutheran and broader Protestant theology, the "Means of Grace" are the specific ways God offers forgiveness and spirit, traditionally defined as the Word of God (the Bible) and the Sacraments.</note> ordained by **GOD**. To be sure, some do not hold the Word and the Sacraments in high regard; however, the most sensible among them hold these in honor. Therefore, one finds no difficulty here.

### §. XXIX.

After meditation or reflection follows beholding or *Contemplation* <term>contemplatio</term> <note>While "meditation" is often seen as an active use of the mind to think about God, "contemplation" is traditionally described by mystics as a more passive, direct "gazing" upon Divine truths.</note>. Here, however, we find more work to do. For the question arises: Why do they distinguish this from *Meditation*, when both mean the same thing? Yet, because it pleased them to use *Contemplation* as a mystical cloak <term>Mäntelchen</term> <note>The author repeats this metaphor from the previous page, suggesting that complex terminology is often used to hide a lack of clarity or to make simple truths seem more "mystical" than they are.</note>, we must investigate the true nature of this *Contemplation*.

### §. XXX.

1) We see that such contemplation follows from *Meditation* and the proper use of the Means of Grace <term>Gnaden-Mittel</term> <note>The Word of God and the Sacraments.</note>, and indeed arises from them. 2) They place this contemplation not only in the intellect <term>Verstand</term> but also in the will <term>Willen</term>. 3) Now, if it resides in the will, it must incline itself toward something good. For it is the nature of the will to incline toward that which is good, or at least what is perceived to be good. This can be nothing other than something from what one has read or heard in the Word and has reflected upon. In that Word, there are two aspects: Law and Gospel <note>The "Law and Gospel" distinction is a cornerstone of Lutheran theology; the Law reveals human sin and God's requirements, while the Gospel offers the "good news" of forgiveness and grace.</note>. The former punishes, threatens, and condemns the sinner; the latter allures, comforts, and refreshes. The former shows hell and damnation;

<summary>The author analyzes "contemplation," questioning why mystical writers treat it as distinct from meditation. He argues that true contemplation must be rooted in the biblical Word, specifically the interaction between the Law (which condemns) and the Gospel (which comforts).</summary>

distinguished and placed after the same. Since it is well known that *adjectives* <note>original: "Adjectiva"</note> indicate a thing distinct from what they name, and there is a great difference between the quality and the thing in which the quality is found.

### §. XXXIV.

However, it is not without reason that <term>contemplation</term> <note>original: "Contemplatio"</note> is called <term>loving</term> <note>original: "amorosa"</note>. For by this, the nature of true faith can be indicated, which is active through love. Thus it is also no wonder that the <term>mystics</term> <note>original: "Mystici"; spiritual writers who focused on the inner experience of God</note> mention faith so sparingly: because it is wrapped up within contemplation.

### §. XXXV.

They have also undoubtedly done this on the occasion of the Scripture, in which "seeing" sometimes means the same as "believing."

### §. XXXVI.

And here we must notice once more that they keep the means through which <term>justification</term> <note>original: "Rechtfertigung"; the theological process by which a sinner is declared righteous by God.</note> and union are obtained obscured and secret. ### §. XXXVII.

Yet we move further, and after we have looked at these means, we also consider the other matters that are placed alongside them. These include <term>passivity</term> <note>original: "passivitas"</note>, <term>a passive state</term> <note>original: "status passivus"</note>, <term>passive prayer</term> <note>original: "oratio passiva"</note>, <term>inaction</term> <note>original: "inactio"</note>, and so forth. This now appears strange enough. However, we still find, as if under a great deal of ash, a tiny spark in which the old truth glimmers. That is to say, something good which harmonizes with our pure doctrine and is grounded in **God's** Word: namely, that in <term>conversion</term> <note>the process of turning toward God</note> and <term>justification</term> <note>the theological act of being declared righteous before God</note>, man conducts himself only passively <note>original: "passive se habeat"; meaning man is a recipient of grace rather than an active participant in earning it</note>. By this, the old, pure <term>mystics</term> <note>spiritual writers who emphasized the direct experience of the divine</note>—before the new ones smeared their filth upon it <note>The author likely refers to contemporary "Quietists" or radical Pietists whom he views as having corrupted older medieval mystical traditions</note>—excluded all human works, merit, and worthiness. This they also sometimes venture to say clearly and distinctly without any disguise. For instance, when a well-known old mystic says: "No one may earn salvation. The service of all creatures is too small."

### §. XXXIX.

Furthermore, they say that they recognize **God** in ignorance and darkness, and that when all the powers of the soul stand bound, **God** pours Himself directly into the soul. This appears to be strange stuff and might make the whole matter suspicious. However, because these are mystical matters and riddles, we must not look at the words, but rather at the underlying meaning <note>original: "Verstand"; here referring to the intended sense or rational core of the statement</note>. That meaning is this: that although by nature we are ignorant and nothing but sheer darkness—we who understand nothing of spiritual matters and stand there as if all our natural powers were bound—**God** can and will nonetheless work within our souls, converting us, justifying us, and uniting Himself with us, without any natural knowledge or understanding on our part.

Thus, they intended to suggest nothing other than what is written in our **Catechism**: **I believe that I cannot, by my own reason or strength, believe in Jesus Christ my Lord, or come to Him;** <note>The author is quoting Martin Luther’s Small Catechism, specifically the explanation of the Third Article of the Apostles' Creed regarding the Holy Spirit.</note> and what **Paul** says: *The natural man perceives nothing* <note>original: "Der natürliche Mensch vernimmt nichts"; a reference to 1 Corinthians 2:14, which explains that spiritual truths cannot be understood through human intellect alone</note>

->### §. XL.<-

"Certainly!" one might say: "that is easily said: but where is the proof?" With **GOD’s** help, I will not remain a debtor to that proof. And because the word "directly" <note>original: "unmittelbar"; refers to unmediated contact between God and the soul, a controversial concept that some feared bypassed the "means of grace" like Scripture and Sacraments</note> makes the matter most suspicious, I will strive to demonstrate that such a term is not detrimental to the truth. 

1) It is indisputable that when explaining words, one must look at the subject matter being discussed. Now, it is obvious that the discussion concerns the natural powers of the soul, which are bound. Thus, we cannot understand the word "directly" in any other way than that the natural powers are excluded—as those things without which **GOD** unites Himself with us. 

2) It is also necessary, when one wishes to judge matters, to consider them according to all circumstances. The <term>legal scholars</term> <note>original: "Rechtsgelehrte"; likely referring to a principle in Roman Law: "Incivile est, nisi tota lege perspecta... judicare" (It is improper to judge without looking at the whole law).</note> hold it to be shameful when someone wishes to judge a law that they have not viewed in its entirety. ->§. XLI.<-

When we now weigh all these things against each other, we see that they represent the right and orderly means to attain <term>Justification</term> <note>The theological process by which a person is made right or "just" in the eyes of God.</note> and **Union** with **GOD** through *Reading, Meditation,* and *Contemplation* <note>These three terms (original Latin: *Lectio, Meditatio, Contemplatio*) refer to the traditional stages of prayer: reading Scripture, thinking deeply upon it, and finally resting in God's presence.</note>: while, on the other hand, they described the wrong means—namely one's own strength, ability, merit, and worthiness—as reprehensible. And because all of this agrees with **GOD'S** Word, we should fairly attribute this intention to the dear ancients in a spirit of love, believing this was their meaning. For it would run contrary to Christian love to try to make them into heretics, especially since one can still harmonize their words with the Scripture. The legal scholars say: *Doubtful matters are to be interpreted in the most favorable light* <note>original Latin: "Dubia in meliorem partem sunt interpretanda"</note>, and, *in obscure matters, we always follow the least burdensome path* <note>original Latin: "semper in obscuris, quod minimum est, sequimur." This is a legal principle suggesting that when a statement is unclear, one should choose the interpretation that is the simplest or least severe.</note>. By which they intend to suggest that in doubtful and obscure things, one should always choose the best and most equitable path. ->§. XLII.<-

Having now examined that which is "something," we also wish to consider the **Nothing**. The <term>Mystics</term> <note>Spiritual writers who focus on the soul's direct, internal union with the Divine.</note> concern themselves so much with this "Nothing" that some people even view it as <term>nonsense</term> <note>original German: *Haseliren*, referring to frivolous, confused, or nonsensical babbling.</note>. Now, I certainly do not intend to excuse those who take the matter too far; yet, if one weighs the matter correctly, something good can be found here as well. When they say: *Man must go into his Nothing, and stand in the Nothing; he can find everything in the Nothing,* and so forth, their meaning is this: When a person deals with **GOD** and stands before His judgment, he must, in heartfelt humility, recognize his own nothingness, incapacity, and unworthiness—that he knows nothing, can do nothing, and possesses nothing by which he might reconcile an angered **GOD** and turn away well-deserved punishment. When he then sees the **Law** <note>In Lutheran theology, the "Law" refers to God's demands and the subsequent recognition of human sin, while the "Gospel" refers to the promise of grace through Christ.</note> standing on one side, which threatens curse and damnation wherever it is not fulfilled, then—provided he does not wish to despair—he will hold to the **Gospel**, which stands on the other side, and seize **Christ** with His merit, and thus through

->§. XLV.<-

In this same way, that Cardinal <note>Likely a reference to a figure like Cardinal Bellarmine or Pierre de Bérulle, often cited in 17th-century devotional literature for their expressions of humility.</note> also viewed "Nothingness" in the crucifix he painted, upon which he wrote, among other things: I am a pure Nothing. From all of this, as it seems to me, one can sufficiently see that in Mystical Theology <note>original: "Theologia Mystica"; the study of direct, subjective experiences of the divine.</note> beautiful and useful things occur, and thus those who brought this matter forward were not exactly fools or heretics. The only question remains: why did those dear elders "creep into corners" <note>This refers to the practice of meeting in secret or private groups (conventicles) rather than in public, official church settings.</note> and let darkness be their covering?

->§. 52.<-

They are commonly accused of having taken such words and modes of expression from pagan writings. Now, it is true that the Egyptians, Plato, Pythagoras, the Stoics, and almost every sect of the philosophers <term>original: "Weltweisen," literally "the world-wise"; a common 18th-century term for secular philosophers</term> used peculiar words and ways of speaking. If they then took something from these sources and used it without detriment to the truth, I do not see what there is to criticize in that. Especially in cases where they had reasons to hide their opinions. However, I believe one will find little in the ancient, pure Mystical Theology that was taken from the philosophers. This can also be seen from what was previously presented,

->§. LIII.<-

Furthermore, they could defend themselves by the example of our Savior, who also often used a secret way of teaching, as can be seen from so many parables. And when JESUS says: **Destroy this temple and in three days I will raise it up,** <note>A reference to John 2:19, where Jesus speaks metaphorically about his body.</note> was that not spoken mystically enough? It was for this reason that they later accused JESUS no less than we now accuse the Mystics <note>original: "Mysticos."</note>.

->§. LIV.<-

But regardless of all this, one sees here nothing more than what they did. I cannot derive from this the *reason* why they did it, which was the original question.

->§. LV.<-

One might perhaps consider that they wished to separate themselves from the academic theologians <term>original: "Schul-Lehrer"; refers to the Scholastic tradition which emphasized logic, dogma, and rigid definitions over personal spiritual experience</term>. These men occupied themselves with much disputing, distinguishing, and subtle questions, with which they plagued the brain and confused the understanding more than they edified it. Meanwhile, the discipline of the will and the taming of desires <note>original: "Begierden"; often referring to worldly or fleshly appetites that distract from the divine.</note> was given little, and often not any,

# Scriptural Consideration

clear consideration. This would certainly be praiseworthy—that they concerned themselves more with a godly life than with hair-splitting <note>original: "Haarkleuberey"; refers to overly trivial or pedantic logical distinctions.</note> and verbal disputes. But did this have to happen with such obscure words? Could one not lead people onto the path of virtue with clear concepts? Or what necessity forced them to hide in the darkness? For one cannot accuse such pious people—as one may well regard the ancient <term>Mystics</term> <note>original: "Mysticos"; those seeking direct, personal experience of the divine presence.</note>—of having chosen this shadowy manner out of fleshly impulse or idle curiosity. Furthermore, it is not merely teachings on virtue that they present. And what most contradicts this opinion is that the <term>Mystics</term> existed long before the Scholastic teachers, as can be proven by the example of the so-called <term>Dionysius the Areopagite</term> <note>original: "Dionysii Areopagitæ"; a highly influential Christian theologian and philosopher of the late 5th to early 6th century.</note>. Although it is uncertain at what time he lived, it is certain that he lived long before <term>Lombard</term> <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), whose work became the standard textbook for medieval "School-theology."</note>, after whom academic bickering <note>original: "Schul-Gezäncke"; refers to the dry, often contentious logical debates of the Scholastic university system.</note> first became rampant.

->§. LVI.<-

### §. LVII.

But if these mysteries are in the Scripture, please tell us as well: whether the **Holy Spirit** presented them skillfully enough, or not? If they say the latter, they offend against the **Holy Spirit** and wish to be wiser than Wisdom itself. It is, to be sure, nothing new that "Wisdom must let herself be justified by her children." <note>original: "daß die Weißheit sich muß rechtfertigen lassen von ihren Kindern"; a reference to the Gospels of Matthew and Luke where Jesus notes that those who truly understand God's work will recognize it, despite critics.</note> f) But what will **Paul** say to that? "Claiming to be wise, they became fools." g) If they must confess, however, that the mysteries are presented skillfully enough by the Holy Spirit, they testify at the same time that all mystical junk <note>original: "Plunder"; refers to worthless or trivial items, suggesting the author views extra-biblical mystical writings as theological clutter.</note> that is presented without and outside of the Scripture is in vain. And thus, instead of a reason, they have told us as good as nothing.

# Scriptural Consideration

->§. LIIX.<-

But since no reasonable person does anything without a cause, the question remains: what is the cause of this obscure language? If I am not mistaken, what was said earlier will set us on the right track. We have seen that primarily justification, and the means to attain it, are draped in "mystical cloaks" <note>The author uses this metaphor to suggest that mystical writers hide simple biblical truths under layers of complex, unnecessary terminology.</note>. Through repentance—which in this context is called *purification*—they lead us (who are to consider ourselves as nothing) and right faith—which they call *contemplation*—toward justification, which is here called *illumination*, upon which *union* depends. Following this is renewal: which they imply when they call contemplation *loving* <note>original: "amorosam"</note>, or indeed when they explicitly require love. And thus <term>Mystical Theology</term> <note>original: "Theologia mystica"; the study of direct, subjective experiences of the divine.</note> encompasses the core of all Christianity, as the doctrine of: 1) repentance, 2) justification, 3) union with **God**, and 4) renewal, or new obedience. However, whatever does not harmonize with the scriptural explanation of these main points does not belong to the ancient mystical tradition; rather, it has been patched onto it by poorly instructed people.

<meta>catchword: §. LIX.</meta>

# Of Mystical Theology.

### §. LIX

Now, since this is the case—and I do not see by what right it could be denied—one easily understands why they invented <term>Mysticism</term> <note>original: "Mysticam"; referring here to a hidden or secretive method of religious instruction.</note> or a secret way of teaching. As previously stated, they have hidden nothing so carefully as the doctrine of justification and the means to attain it. It is well known that the article of justification, being the very heart of Christianity, has always stood as the primary target for the Devil’s malice, cunning, and power, as he seeks to corrupt and hinder it. What Christ had to contend with regarding the Pharisees, and what the Apostles faced with the <term>legalists</term> <note>original: "Wercklehrer"; literally "work-teachers," referring to those who teach that salvation is earned through human deeds and rituals rather than through faith.</note>, is well known from God’s Word. What happened in the ages following is taught by Church History. For although there has been no lack of upright teachers who insisted upon the righteousness of faith and rejected its opposite, they were nonetheless unable to stop the weeds. These weeds multiplied to such an extent that they nearly choked the good grain. Because it was no longer safe to promote this truth publicly, they began to consider ways and means to preserve the right doctrine of the justification of a sinner before God,

->§. LX.<-

This they practiced in secret with those yearning for spiritual comfort. And so, like <term>Nicodemus</term> and <term>Joseph of Arimathea</term> <note>original: "Nicodemo und Josepho von Arimathia"; New Testament figures who were "secret disciples" of Jesus for fear of the religious authorities.</note>, they were disciples of Christ in hiding. Just as those biblical figures hid for fear of the Jewish authorities, so these individuals hid for fear of the <term>legalists</term> <note>original: "Wercklehrern"; literally "work-teachers," referring to those who taught that salvation is earned through human rituals and deeds rather than faith.</note>. These were men who looked more to human laws, ceremonies, and superstitious dealings than to God's Word; they trusted in their own works, merit, and worthiness more than in the merit of Christ, and they followed the saints more than they followed Christ. The hidden believers acted in this way so long as no public confession of faith was demanded of them. For when it came to that, they were not afraid to confess publicly and to give up their lives for Christ, as the examples of so many Christians killed by the <term>Antichrist</term> <note>A term often used in Reformation-era writing to describe institutional church power that the author believes has turned against the true spirit of the Gospel.</note> bear witness.

As to who the first authors and originators of this [theology] were, we shall search in vain...

### §. LXII.

Now, while this teaching was propagated for a certain time—which cannot be precisely specified—it finally came to be written down. But in doing so, they sent the husks <note>The author uses a metaphor: the "husk" or "shell" represents the difficult, outward words of mystical writing, while the "kernel" is the actual spiritual meaning.</note> out into the world, while they kept the kernel or the explanation at home. Whoever then wished to know the right understanding had to hold to someone who was experienced in it. From this it comes that, even to this day, the <term>Mystics</term> <note>original: "Mystici"; those who seek a direct, internal experience of God’s presence.</note> direct their students to a <term>Father Confessor</term> <note>original: "Beicht-Vater"; a priest who hears confession and offers spiritual guidance.</note>, a <term>Counselor of Conscience</term> <note>original: "Gewissens-Rath"; an advisor for spiritual and moral matters.</note>, a spiritual father, and so forth.

# of Mystical Theology.

The purpose of their endeavor is, and nothing else, but **union with God** <note>original: "Vereinigung mit GOtt"; often referred to in theology as the *unio mystica*, the goal of the contemplative life.</note>. That is, that we do not sin knowingly and intentionally; but always guard ourselves so that, according to **Tobias's advice**, we do not consent to any sin and act against God’s commandment <note>A reference to the Book of Tobit 4:5: "My son, be mindful of the Lord our God all thy days, and let not thy will be set to sin, or to transgress his commandments."</note>.

->§. LXXIII.<-

But they will claim that they say such things only under a certain condition: if **God** should wish it so. Now, this condition is either possible in their thoughts, or it is not. But if they consider this condition possible—that **God** could or would cast His true lover <note>In this context, "lover" (*Liebhaber*) refers to a person who possesses a sincere, reciprocal love for God.</note> into hell—then they blaspheme **God** by portraying Him as unjust and a liar. For **God** has said: *He who believes shall be saved.* <note>original: "Wer gläubet, wird selig" (Mark 16:16).</note> A true lover of **God** believes; for true love comes from faith alone. Therefore, a lover of **God** must also be saved. And **God** cannot, without violating His promise, cast His lover into hell for the sake of the faith from which that love flows. And so, no person can say with a good conscience that they wish to be damned out of love for **God**. For to do so would be to attribute unjust things to **God**. Even a pagan <term>Pagan (German: *Heyde*): The author likely refers to ancient Greek or Roman philosophers who argued that the gods, by definition, must be perfectly just.</term> understands well enough that one must not ask anything unjust of a righteous person.

### §. LXXIV.

<margin>d) Exodus 32:33.</margin>

They <note>Referring to the "Mystics" discussed on the previous page.</note> must not appeal to Moses and Paul. If examples are to prove anything, they must be consistent and validated by God's Word. These examples fall short of that. Both the impulse and the purpose are different. With Moses and Paul, the wish arose from heartfelt love and compassion toward their neighbor. The *Mystics* cannot boast of this. Indeed, it is well known that those who wish to be the most spiritual and holy hold the love of the neighbor in no regard. <note>The author critiques a branch of mysticism (often associated with Quietism) that emphasized a private, internal relationship with God to the exclusion of external Christian duties and charity.</note> Moses and Paul had this objective: that many thousands of people might be saved from destruction. d) This reason must also hold true against Paul, whose request is considered equal to that of Moses. <note>In Romans 9:3, Paul writes that he could wish himself "accursed from Christ" for the sake of his fellow Israelites.</note> For it is not at all unusual for holy people to err at times. There are examples enough of this in the Scripture.

# Scriptural Reflection

Moreover, all the reasons previously mentioned are of such a nature that examples cannot accomplish anything against them. Yet, it seems to me that the matter is easily resolved if one simply compares the works of the **Mystics** <note>original: *Mysticorum*</note> against their words. If the Eternal were a matter of indifference to them, then temporal matters ought to be all the more indifferent. However, two great masters of the **Mystical guild** have demonstrated the exact opposite. One held his life, and the other his **Archbishopric**, as anything but indifferent; instead, both used every possible means to retain them. <note>The author likely refers to the "Quietist" controversy. The "Archbishop" is almost certainly François Fénelon (Archbishop of Cambrai), who defended his mystical writings against Bossuet and the Pope to keep his standing.</note> This occurred, even though it could not be done without detriment to the **Truth**. There is indeed such a great difference between saying and doing.

->§. LXXV.<-

From this **abuse of love** also flows the idea that they wish neither to ask **GOD** for anything, nor to thank Him for anything. For they believe that to do so would make their love "interested." <note>The Quietist doctrine of "disinterested love" argued that one should love God for His own sake, without desire for reward or fear of punishment, leading some to stop asking God for help or even salvation.</note> Though others believe that such [requests] are a form of **self-will**, which they claim must be surrendered. But what does **GOD** say to this? *Call upon me in the day of trouble; I will deliver you, and you shall honor me.* <note>Psalm 50:15</note> This must carry more weight than human **subtleties** <note>original: *Klügeln* - refers to overly complex or "clever" reasoning that misses the simple truth of Scripture.</note>.

# Scriptural Contemplation

[...recognize] as guild-associates? <note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding whether radical mystics would accept other controversial figures into their spiritual "guild."</note> For surely they are no worse than the heathens? They certainly want to love **GOD**, yet they do not want to know what it is they love; they refuse to grasp any certain concept of Him in their intellect—whether He is kind, merciful, almighty, wise, and so forth—but instead, everything is supposed to happen in darkness and ignorance. **GOD** is supposed to be all in all, and yet nothing in anyone. ### §. LXXIIX.

The "binding of the soul-powers" <term>Seelen-Kräffte; the traditional faculties of the soul, such as reason, memory, and will</term> and the "passive state" <term>Passivum; a state of quietude where the soul ceases its own activity to let God act within it</term>, which the ancients <note>Referring to the early Church Fathers or respected medieval theologians.</note> used in a sound sense, they fundamentally corrupt. They lead these concepts toward matters that are not only impossible but even blasphemous. In such a state, they claim **God** pours Himself into the soul in such a way that the intellect experiences nothing of it. The soul is to be annihilated <note>original: *annihiliret*</note>, absorbed <note>original: *absorbiret*</note>, and deified <note>original: *vergöttert*</note>...

of Mystical Theology.

<meta>catchword: -ly</meta>

[...be annihilated.] And yet, such a person supposedly could not only have evil thoughts, but also perform evil works. They claim one should not resist these <note>This refers to the controversial Quietist doctrine that resisting temptation is a form of "self-will" that interferes with God's work; they argued one should remain passive even during impure thoughts.</note>, because the soul is supposedly purified thereby, and so forth. That is what one might call "passing right through" <note>original: *durchhin kommen*; likely a sarcastic idiom meaning to find a convenient loophole or to justify anything.</note>. It is impossible in a natural and orderly way (for what **GOD** can or will do extraordinarily is not the question here) that the will be acted upon without the intellect. The intellect must be enlightened beforehand, either naturally or supernaturally, as the Scholastics <term>Schul-Lehrer; the traditional academic theologians of the universities</term> and philosophers <term>Weltweisen; literally "world-wise ones," referring to those who study natural reason</term> have long since pointed out to them, by their own admission. It also runs contrary to their own doctrines. Indeed, they make much fuss about their "experience." Such experience is impossible without knowledge <note>original: *Erkäntniß*</note>, yet this knowledge resides in the intellect.

### §. LXXIX.

And how is it possible that a person can remain in devotion at all times with "bound powers" <note>The mystical state where the soul's natural faculties—reason, memory, and will—are "bound" or suspended so that God alone may act.</note>? And do they not contradict themselves when they say that a person in the passive <note>original: *leidentli-* [leidentlichen Stand]; the text breaks off here mid-word, referring to the "passive state" where the soul is supposedly a silent vessel for the divine.</note>—

[...Father, how a person in such a passi]ve state (*statu passivo* <note>original Latin: *statu passivo*; a theological term for a state of total surrender where the soul's own faculties are silent so God can act through them</note>) can have evil thoughts and be able to sin?

### §. LXXX.

In the same way, it is impossible that the soul should be *annihilated* <note>original: *annihiliret*; to be reduced to nothingness, a key concept in some radical mystical traditions where the "self" must cease to exist to unite with God</note> and yet be swallowed up. *Jonah* was swallowed up, but he was not *annihilated*, as the outcome proved: for how could the fish have spit him out again if he had been nothing?

### §. LXXXI.

And if the soul has not become nothing, how can it be **GOD** or become deified <note>original: *vergöttert*; the process of being made divine or reaching union with God</note>? That is nonsensical and impossible. But it is blasphemous to suggest that a soul, having been deified, should be able to sin. For **GOD** and sin stand infinitely apart from one another. This is something not only **GOD's** Word, but also sound reason can teach us.

### §. LXXXII.

Is it not also blasphemous to suggest that the purification of souls can be promoted through sins? Can one wipe away filth with manure? What is attributed in the Scripture to *Christ*, to his blood, and to faith, sin is supposed to accomplish according to them. Is that not horrific?

### §. LXXXIII.

### §. LXXXVII.

A Papist *Mystic* <note>a period-specific, often polemical term for a Roman Catholic practitioner of mystical theology</note>, insofar and as long as he is such a one, does not seek to be justified through faith. Otherwise, the **Holy Spirit** would have to become a liar. Which is, however, impossible.

Where true experience exists, there is also peace and joy in the **Holy Spirit**, as these qualities depend upon righteousness m) and therefore cannot be separated from justification. And although these may well be kept hidden during times of affliction and temptation—when the **Bridegroom of the soul** stands "behind the wall" n) <note>a reference to Christ's perceived absence or distance during spiritual trials</note>—they nevertheless reveal themselves at the final departure, so that the person united with **GOD**, like Simeon <note>the biblical figure who, having seen the infant Jesus, declared he could now "depart in peace"</note>, may pass away in peace. From where, then, should the experience come? Or in what should it consist?

# Scriptural Contemplation

### §. XCV.

Since there is no need in this manner to crawl into the darkness, it is only right to guard oneself against mystical writings. And this is all the more necessary because the good teachings that might be found in them, and which people use as their authority, can also be found among our own teachers—and indeed, much more clearly. Moreover, in the Word of **God**, we have everything necessary for our salvation <term>Seligkeit: the state of eternal happiness or being saved in heaven</term> clearly enough. This does not deceive us. It is therefore better that we spend the time that others devote to mystical matters on the contemplation of Holy Scripture.

### §. XCVI.

And we could say all of this even if mystical theology <note>original: "die Mystica"; referring to the tradition of mystical writings</note> had remained pure and stayed close to Holy Scripture until now. Because that which is clear and distinct is always more useful than what is dark and incomprehensible. How much more so now, when a literal monster has been made out of the ancient secret teaching through false interpretation and wicked additions. Now, the reading of mystical things is highly dangerous. It is like sugar mixed with arsenic <note>original: "Mäuse-Pulver" (mouse powder); a term for rodent poison, often containing arsenic</note>, which one could advise no person to consume. O, how easily those who do not have senses trained to distinguish between evil and good can be led astray!

<meta>catchword: §. XCVII.</meta>

<summary>The author warns against reading mystical texts, arguing that any "good" within them is already found more clearly in Protestant teachings. He likens these "dark" writings to poison hidden in sugar, capable of easily deceiving the spiritually untrained.</summary>
<keywords>mystical writings, Holy Scripture, clarity vs. obscurity, salvation, spiritual danger, discernment</keywords>

### §. XCVII.

Even the learned themselves, if they do not handle these matters cautiously, can be led astray. O, how much better it would be for our Church if we could point to no examples of this! When people teach that conversion begins with the human will; that enlightenment only follows after conversion; that the knowledge of the unconverted is carnal or even devilish; that the calling <note>The "calling" or *Beruf* refers to God's invitation to salvation.</note> happens without the external Word <term>External Word: the physical Bible or the spoken sermon, as opposed to direct revelation to the soul</term>; that one must be guided by the inner word, and so forth. Are these not significant errors? Yet such things were not set in motion or defended by the unlearned, but rather by some who are highly learned.

### §. XCVIII.

Where, then, do these errors come from? From the incorrect explanation of mystical matters. If they had correctly viewed—or wished to understand according to Scripture—the threefold mystical path <note>The "threefold path" (lat. *via triplex*) consists of Purgation (cleansing), Illumination (enlightenment), and Union (oneness with God).</note>, from which the first three errors arose; and the word "Immediate," from which the last two come, they would never have arrived at such thoughts. These ideas are not only unscriptural; in part, they are not even rational.

### §. XCIX.

Why, then, do we want to venture into such danger? Yes, why do we want to dishonor the Word of God—which is an infallible light—and be so ungrateful to the gracious God, who wants to bring us to the light through light, by crawling into the dark and thus loving the darkness more than the light? Why do we not consider that it is very easy for God to knock over our lampstand <note>A reference to Revelation 2:5, where God warns a church that He will remove its "lampstand"—its status and spiritual life—if it does not repent.</note> and bring us back to a point where we may not even publicly confess the righteousness of faith? Or could it not also happen to us what happened to the seven churches in Asia <note>The seven churches mentioned in the biblical book of Revelation (chapters 2–3), which serve as historical warnings of spiritual decline.</note>, and in more recent times to many churches in various countries? Geist: Holy Spirit (Heiliger Geist).</term> who dwells in us, and devoutly pray to God that He may keep His Word pure and clear for us and our descendants until the end of the world, check all heretical darknesses, and strengthen us in true faith, so that we may carry away the goal of our faith, namely the

# Preface.

Some will perhaps wonder why I am meddling in **mystical matters** <note>In this context, "mystical matters" refers to internal, spiritual experiences of God, which were often viewed with suspicion by the institutional Church of the 17th and 18th centuries.</note>, considering the conflicting judgments that are passed regarding them. Especially since one could easily fall into disfavor with both parties <note>The "both parties" likely refers to the strict Lutheran Orthodox theologians on one side and the radical spiritualists or Pietists on the other.</note>. I might almost wonder at it myself, as one who in my younger years concerned myself little with the **mystics** and held them in low regard. This was caused as much by certain prejudices, as

This was so that I would not only have a certain understanding <term>understanding (Begriff): A clear conceptual grasp or definition of a subject</term> of it for myself, but could also instruct my listeners about it when necessary.

For what is more glorious than a perfect blessedness <note>original: *Glückseeligkeit* — a term implying both supreme happiness and spiritual salvation.</note>, which arises from the **union with God** and the **enjoyment of God**. # Preface.

While this word could indeed have a sound meaning, it is rightly avoided because it is used by some in an erroneous sense and is not to be found in the **Holy Scripture**. If one examines the means they propose for reaching this goal, they are varied and, in part, sound very strange and foreign. But the most common means, which no <term>mystic (Mysticus): a person seeking direct, transformative experience of the divine presence</term> will reject, is <term>contemplation (Contemplation): the act of silent, prayerful "beholding" or focused spiritual gazing upon God</term> or spiritual beholding.

# Preface.

->A 4<-

but [since I] reflected on the matter somewhat more closely, I found that between **mystical** and *Platonic* *contemplation* there is a very great difference, and thus the word must have been taken from somewhere else other than from *Plato*. Firstly, it is well-known that the *Platonists* view their *contemplation* as an end <note>original: *Zweck* — a final goal or purpose.</note> in itself, whereas the **mystics** <note>original: *Mystici*</note> use it as a means to reach the end. This is a palpable difference. Furthermore, it is also not to be denied that **mystical** *contemplation* resides partly in the intellect <note>original: *Verstande*</note> and partly in the will, whereas the *Platonic* resides in the intellect alone. For this very reason, the **philosophers** <note>original: *Weltweisen* — literally "world-wise ones," a term used for secular or pagan scholars of philosophy.</note> in **ethics** <note>original: *Sitten-Lehr* — the study of moral conduct and virtue.</note> do not wish to recognize *contemplation* as the **highest good** <note>original: *höchste Gut*; the ultimate purpose of human life, known in Latin as the *summum bonum*.</note>. Although this difference [to me]

<meta>catchword: very</meta>

<summary>The author distinguishes between Christian mystical contemplation and Platonic contemplation, arguing that the former involves the heart and will as a means to reach God, while the latter is a purely intellectual end in itself. # Jesus Lead Me!

-> §. 1. <-

Only the infinite **Divine Being** <note>original: *Göttliche Wesen*. Refers to the essence of God as the only uncreated and limitless entity.</note> is immeasurable. Everything that is found outside of the Divine Being is enclosed within its own limits and has its own measure. For every thing, a circle is drawn, as it were, in which it is contained, and beyond whose boundary it should not wander. Each thing is also made capable of fulfilling its purpose within that circle. Otherwise, such measure would not be necessary, nor of any use, as **God** has made nothing in vain. Because there are many different things, and they are unequal to one another, their measures must also be manifold and distinct. Yet every thing is perfect in itself, even if one is smaller than another. A rose bush does not grow as thick or as high as an oak tree or a linden tree: A

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the first section, establishing a theological foundation: while God is infinite and immeasurable, all created things have specific, God-given limits and purposes tailored to their nature.</summary>

<keywords>Divine Being, infinity, creation, natural order, God's will, perfection, teleology</keywords>

### Short Reflection

nightingale <note>Continuing from the previous page: "A rose bush does not grow as thick or as high as an oak tree or a linden tree: A [nightingale]..."</note> is not as large as an ostrich. And yet the former are no less perfect than the latter: because the former fulfill their measure and their prescribed circle just as well as the latter. For if one wishes to judge the perfection of a thing, one must not compare it with other matters, but with its own prescribed measure.

->§. II.<-

Because this is now true of all things, it must also apply to **Christianity**. Because it is not infinite, it must also have its measure <term>measure (original: *Maaſſe*): The specific limits or standards set by God for a thing's existence or behavior.</term>. And since that measure was not prescribed to it in vain, it must also be able to fulfill it. When this occurs, it must be perfect. **Reason** can reach no other judgment, with which **God’s Word** also agrees. For we read in the same about many who are praised for being perfect. a) It also urges the—

### §. III.

Such wandering of proud thoughts occurs when people imagine, and allow themselves to be convinced, that they can be gods or become deified <term>deified (original: *vergöttert*): The mystical idea that a human could literally become God or take on divine nature, which the author rejects here.</term>. Healthy **reason** can view this as nothing other than absurd. And in **God’s Word**, it also has no foundation. What is brought forward from Scripture may well prove a union with **God**, but not a transformation into **God**, or a deification. We must admit that there is a great difference between what we have here <note>Referring to our current earthly life.</note> and what we have there <note>Referring to the afterlife or heaven.</note>. But even there, we will not become gods or be deified. On what basis can we then expect to become gods here? We will indeed become like Him there; but that this does not imply deification is evident from what follows: *We shall see Him as He is.* <note>A reference to 1 John 3:2: "We know that when He is revealed, we shall be like Him, for we shall see Him as He is."</note> The words of the **Apostle** and **Evangelist**

# Brief Reflection

->§. VII.<-

But we must measure such [perfection] according to our current state, according to the wretched Fall into Sin <note>The "Fall into Sin" refers to the biblical narrative of Adam and Eve's first disobedience, which the author argues permanently damaged human nature.</note>, and be content with such a perfection that indeed loves no sin, nor allows sin to rule over it, yet nevertheless cannot be entirely without sin. We must also look toward the purpose of our pilgrimage <note>The "pilgrimage" (original: *Wallfahrt*) is a common metaphorical term for the Christian's journey through life toward heaven.</note>. Whatever can bring us to that destination, we may well regard as our perfection. Holy Scripture is praised as perfect. This is not because everything that could ever be known, in time and eternity, is contained within it; rather, it is because it can instruct and lead us to eternal life, and **GOD** has revealed everything in it that is necessary for eternal life. Likewise, Christianity can also be called perfect, because through it we can come to eternal life, and nothing further is required on our part.

->§. VIII.<- <note>The original uses the archaic Roman numeral IIX for VIII (8).</note>

->§. IX.<-

Which perfect means is required to attain perfection and perfect bliss in this life likely requires a more exact and cautious inquiry. This is especially true since the <term>Mystics (original: *Mystici*): Spiritual writers and thinkers who emphasized internal experience and direct union with God, often through rigorous contemplative practices.</term> point to so many various paths and yet must confess that their work often fails. Likewise, bliss in the Kingdom of Grace and in the Kingdom of Glory is attained through the very same means.

### §. XII.

It also does not hinder anything if faith is weak. A person, though he may have a fever or another sickness, is nevertheless still a complete human being so long as the soul is within him. A child and an old man stand in a state of great inequality: for a child cannot do what an old man can do. Yet, a child is just as much a complete human being as an adult, because it possesses a **rational soul.** <note>original: *vernünfftige Seele*. In historical theology, the "rational soul" is the essential spark of humanity that exists regardless of physical strength or age.</note> Likewise, he is a **perfect Christian** who has a righteous faith, even if the fruits of that faith are unequal. <note>The author argues that "perfection" is about the presence of genuine faith, not the quantity of good works or "fruits" one produces.</note>

### §. XIII.

However, so that we do not grasp **coals instead of treasure,** <note>A proverbial expression meaning to be deceived by a false appearance or to mistake something worthless for something precious.</note> but rather learn to know faith correctly, we must observe:
1) What precedes faith.
2) What faith itself is.
3) What follows from and upon faith.

### §. XVII.

<margin>r) Luke 5:31.</margin>
It must be so. Such fear and anxiety must precede faith, otherwise one will not concern themselves with Christ. Christ says: *Those who are healthy do not need a physician, but those who are sick.* r) These are the ones who feel sin in their conscience. However, all such repentance, such sorrow, such fear, and such anxiety—whether it be called **attrition** <note>original: "Attritio"; in theology, this refers to "imperfect" repentance motivated by fear of punishment.</note> or **contrition** <note>original: "Contritio"; "perfect" repentance motivated by a sincere love for God.</note>—can earn nothing from God. Can even the most intense pain of a sick person "earn" their health? Just as little can the anguish of the conscience, no matter how piercing it may be, earn salvation and eternal blessedness. Meanwhile, the pains can drive the sick person to the physician, so that he asks, pleads, cries out, seeks help, and gladly accepts it. Thus, for whoever feels sin in their conscience, the preaching of the Gospel is sweet and pleasant, like medicine to the sick, food to the hungry, and drink to the thirsty. Then, one gladly believes the Gospel, which reveals comfort and help.

...more detailed, yet as briefly as possible. If we wish to have a description of faith, we find one in Hebrews 11:1: *Faith is a certain confidence in that which one hopes for, and a state of not doubting that which one does not see.* From this, we gather that faith requires: 1) a knowledge of the things that one is meant to believe. No one concerns themselves with unknown things. Faith, as we see here, is to deal with those matters which are hoped for. Can I hope for something if I do not know whether it exists, or what it is? 

And why do we believe? Is it not so that we may find salvation? <note>German: "selig"; this term describes the state of being "blessed" or "saved" in eternity.</note> Therefore, I must know that there is such a thing as salvation and eternal life. From whom do we receive eternal life? From God. Thus, we must indeed know that there is a God, and who He is according to His essence, His persons, <note>A reference to the three persons of the Trinity: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</note> His attributes, and His works. 

What, then, moves God to desire our salvation? It is not our own merit or worthiness, for we have deserved punishment because of our sins; rather, He does it out of grace and mercy. Thus, we must have word of this. We could not enjoy such mercy of His, if...

In 17th-century theology, faith was seen to have three parts: *notitia* (knowledge of facts), *assensus* (assent or agreement that those facts are true), and *fiducia* (personal trust). The author is arguing that knowing the facts is not enough without 'assent'.</note> and hold it to be an infallible truth. Otherwise, nothing is accomplished with mere knowledge. ### §. XXVII.

Blessed is the person who has such confidence; they will not be put to shame. Whoever possesses such faith belongs to the "perfect ones" of whom **Paul** speaks in **1 Corinthians 2:6 and Philippians 3:15.** <note>The author is referencing the Greek concept of 'teleioi' used by Paul, which refers to spiritual maturity or being "fully grown" in faith, rather than being without flaw.</note> For in those passages, by "the perfect," we can understand none other than upright, believing Christians. These are people who not only knew what they ought to know about **Christ** and the **Gospel**; but also accepted what they knew as heavenly truth, placed their trust in **Christ**, and sought their well-being, life, and salvation <term>Seligkeit|A state of eternal happiness or "blessedness," referring here to the ultimate goal of the Christian life.</term> in Him alone, so that nothing more was lacking for them that was necessary to obtain eternal life.

### §. XXVIII.

But here too we must be cautious, so that we do not imagine—or allow ourselves to be told—as if the work of faith itself makes us saved, or even the act of "grasping" the merit <term>Verdienst|The legal or moral "credit" earned by Jesus through his life and death, which is applied to the believer.</term> of **JESUS CHRIST**, insofar as it is an act of grasping: but rather the merit of **JESUS CHRIST** alone, which, being confidently grasped—

<summary>This page defines spiritual "perfection" not as sinlessness, but as a mature and upright faith that relies entirely on Christ for salvation. The author cautions that it is Christ's merit, not the human act of believing itself, that saves a person.</summary>

### §. XXIX.

Now that we have considered faith in itself, we must also see what follows from it and upon it. Faith leads us partly toward future things and partly toward present things. The former happens through **hope**, the latter through **love**. Without hope, faith cannot possibly exist, if it is of the right kind. For as soon as faith grasps **Christ** and His merit <term>Verdienst|The spiritual "credit" or legal standing earned by Jesus through his life and death, which is credited to the believer.</term>, and appropriates it as a present reality and holds it fast, hope immediately rises to **GOD** and grasps **GOD’s** love, protection, and eternal life. These are the treasures earned through **Christ’s** merit. For because the believer has **Christ** through faith, he hopes that, as a **co-heir** with **Christ**, he will in due time also have a share in eternal life. And because he hopes for this, as the greater thing, he does not doubt that **GOD** will also give him the lesser things, which pertain to

### §. XXX.

In this way, **faith** and **hope** are firmly linked; and yet, they are not one and the same. **Faith** grasps **Christ** himself along with his merit <term>Verdienst|The spiritual 'credit' or legal standing Jesus earned, which is given to the believer.</term>; but **hope** grasps the benefit and the fruit of the merit of **JESUS Christ**. **Faith** has its object—namely, **Christ** with his righteousness <term>Gerechtigkeit|A state of being right with God, not through one's own deeds, but through Christ's perfection.</term>—as a present reality. **Hope**, however, deals with future, and thus still absent, things. Because hope presents these things to itself as certainly as if they were already present, it also enjoys the benefit of the future even now in the present, and takes comfort in the future as if it were already there. This can be seen in the saints of the **Old Testament**, of whom it is said that they saw the promise from afar and comforted themselves with it, being well content. g) And what else made the **martyrs** so courageous but the **hope** of future salvation <note>original: 'Seligkeit'. ### §. XXXVI.

The love, however, which is required of us, must not be blind, but must have eyes. That is, whoever wishes to love must rightly know himself and that which he wishes to, or ought to, love. Whoever does not rightly know **GOD** can also not rightly love Him, nor place any right trust in Him—which is found alongside true love. Whoever knows **GOD**, and realizes that He is such a loving, kind, just, and almighty **GOD**, should catch fire with love toward Him in such a way that nothing would seem too difficult for him to do or to suffer. Should those who live so boldly from day to day, following after all manner of sins, truly know God—that He is holy, just, almighty, and truthful? Or should they even know, or want to know, that there is a **God**? And although they boast of their knowledge, it remains true what **Paul** says: They say they know **GOD**, but with their works they deny Him, since they are those in whom **God** finds abomination, and they do not obey, and are unfit for any good works. <margin>r) Titus 1:16. <note>original: "Tit. I, 16." This verse describes people who claim to know God but whose actions prove otherwise.</note></margin> r)

### §. XXXIIX.

Knowing oneself is one of the most necessary things. The lack of <term>self-knowledge</term> is the cause of almost all sins. If man recognized that he is a <term>creature</term> of **GOD**, who could break him as easily as He made him, would such a handful of earth then dare to act against the commandment of such a great and powerful **GOD**? When we sin, we forget all too much what we are—we poor <term>earthworms</term> <note>A common historical metaphor emphasizing human frailty and insignificance compared to the Creator.</note>, however great we may [appear to] be!

### §. XLVI.

Love must also be pure, so that it proceeds from a heart and conscience purified by the blood of Christ, unstained by any fleshly or worldly impurity. For how could an impure love be pleasing to the holiest and purest Being, who is **GOD**?

### §. XLVII.

The <term>Mystics</term> <note>original: "Die Mystischen," likely referring to contemporary 17th or 18th-century spiritual writers such as the Quietists, who focused heavily on the internal state of the soul.</note> also chatter much about a "pure love." They consider such love to be pure when it is not <term>interested</term> <note>original: "interessiret," used here in the theological sense of "disinterested love" (*amour désintéressé*), a love for God that seeks no reward, not even salvation.</note>, or when it seeks no benefit. However, they push this idea to an extreme. To be sure, love should not be selfish. Yet, it cannot be held against love if it looks toward an honorable and lawful benefit, provided it remains in subordination to God.

### §. XLVIII.

And when they say that love should not look at benefit at all, I might choose the opposite and say that it is impossible for true <note>original: "rechtschaffene," meaning genuine or rightly-ordered.</note> love to be, or ever to have been, without an intention toward a fitting benefit <term>benefit</term> <note>original: "Nußen." In this theological context, it refers to the spiritual reward, utility, or "fruit" one receives from loving God.</note>. **GOD** and nature indeed do nothing without a certain purpose. Should love, then, which considered in and of itself is also from **GOD**, be able to exist without having a purpose? And what should stand as its purpose: harm or benefit? The answer is easy. What is love other than an inclination of our souls toward that which is good, or at least is held to be good? But why does it incline there? Is it not to unite itself with the same and to enjoy the same? When it now attains this, it cannot happen without delight and pleasure. Even if there is no other benefit present, the enjoyment and the resulting delight and pleasure are already benefit enough. What shall we say of the love of **GOD**? Is it pure or impure?
