### §. LI.

However, let us dismiss such people <note>The author refers to certain mystical thinkers who claimed that any desire for reward or "benefit" (Nutzen) made love for God impure.</note>, for we know that God can well tolerate that we find a benefit and a delight in love, provided we do not seek these things without, against, or above God. Instead, we should arrange everything so that God remains the highest purpose, and that in all things His will and His glory are regarded. This is surely concluded from the promises that God presents in His Word, where He lures us to Himself through temporal and eternal goods. Of what use would these be if love were not permitted to look upon such gifts of grace? Or do we wish to make God a sinner by suggesting He presents us with things that we are not allowed to look upon? Or do we view the divine promises as a mere conjuring trick <note>original: "Gauckel-Spiel." A deceptive puppet show or a magician's illusion.</note> with which He intends to mock us? Since one cannot say such things, one must confess that love can still be pleasing to God even when it regards a benefit <term>benefit</term> <note>original: "Nutzen." Here referring to the spiritual rewards and peace that follow a life of faith.</note>, provided it stays within the proper order. And if it pleases Him, it is pure enough.

## §. LII.

Anyone who wishes to see even more characteristics of love need only read 1 Corinthians 13. Here, we merely note further that love must also be active <note>The author uses the term "thätig," emphasizing that faith and love are not merely internal feelings but must result in outward activity.</note>, as John teaches: g) My little children, let us not love with words, nor with the tongue, but in deed and in truth. Where one loves only with words, love is in a poor state. Where true love is in the heart, it breaks out into works. For the love of Christ compels a person to do good. h) Where there is fire, it soon makes itself noticed through its glow or at least through its heat. For this reason, love is compared to a fire, because it cannot be hidden. Therefore, John says: i) Whoever keeps God’s word, in him the love of God is perfected. However, there are three ways through which love proves itself active: 1. Refraining, 2. Performing, 3. Enduring <note>original: "1. Lassen, 2. Leisten, 3. Leiden." This is a rhetorical triplet common in German devotional literature, categorizing the Christian life into what one stops doing (sin), what one starts doing (service), and what one patiently bears (trials).</note>.

### §. LVIII.

<margin>q) Matthew 7:12.</margin>
Nevertheless, even within nature itself, certain means can be found through which we might arrive at a knowledge of evil. A properly ordered **self-love** <note>original: "Eigen-Liebe." In this context, it refers to a healthy, natural instinct for self-preservation and well-being, which the author argues serves as a moral baseline for how to treat others.</note> provides good instruction here. Every person loves themselves in such a way that they do not want anything harmful to happen to them from others. Since other people are exactly what we are, and are thus our equals—and since one must make the same judgment for equals and show the same treatment to equals—it follows by natural consequence that no evil should befall one's neighbor, whether from others or from ourselves. It is from this foundation that the saying flowed, which was known even to the **heathens** <note>original: "Heyden." Referring to the non-Christian philosophers of antiquity who recognized basic moral truths through natural reason.</note>: *What you do not want done to yourself, do not do to another.* <note>original Latin: "Quod tibi non vis fieri, alteri ne feceris." This is often called the "Negative Golden Rule."</note> This is also approved by Christ our **Savior** <note>original: "Heyland."</note>: *Everything that you want people to do for you, you should also do for them.* q) This is a brief summary of the **Divine Law**, as Christ adds in that place: *This is the Law and the Pro-*

Oh, how necessary it would be to put oneself in the place of one's neighbor! Oh, how many sins would be left undone if this were to happen!

# Short Consideration

Every person who is not entirely wicked, or even a devil, will not do evil publicly; namely, in the presence of the authorities, parents, preachers, and confessors <note>original: "Beicht-Väter." Others crawl morally into corners by cloaking, covering, and excusing their works in all sorts of ways. Which is already proof that the works are no good. Good works require no excuse. That which requires much excuse is already betrayed by that very fact.

# Of Christian Perfection

<margin>s) Ps. 139.</margin>
If, then, a temptation or opportunity to sin presents itself, ask yourself this: Imagine if your superiors, your confessor <note>original: "Beicht-Vater." A spiritual guide who hears confessions and provides moral direction.</note>, your parents, or your master were present right now; would you want to, or even dare to, do this thing? And if the presence of such people holds us back, should not the omnipresence <term>omnipresence: the theological concept that God is present everywhere at all times</term> of God do so much more? For no man can hide from Him, even if he could crawl into the depths of the sea or down into Hell itself. s) Would not many sins be left undone if one always thought upon the presence of God? We often lead our children in prayer, saying: "Fear God, dear child, for God sees every sin." It is only to be lamented that the elders themselves do not keep this in mind. But this will serve to bring about their much greater condemnation.

### §. 62.

When a person uses due caution, breaks their **self-will** <term>self-will: the egoistic desire to act according to one's own impulses rather than according to God's will</term>, bridles their desires, and does not allow themselves to be led astray by delusion and imaginings, they can certainly guard themselves against external, coarse sins and vices. As proof of this, I set forth the examples of the **pagans** <note>original: "Heyden." In this context, the author refers to the virtuous non-Christians of Greek and Roman antiquity.</note>. ->§. LXVII.<-

And here begins the labor of the Christian against **sin**. Here begins the struggle between the **Spirit** and the **flesh**. The "struggling flesh," of which the Scripture speaks, is **original sin** <term>original sin: In German "Erb-Sünde," referring to the inherited fallen nature of humanity.</term> and the lusts arising from it. b) By the "Spirit," however, are understood the spiritual powers given to us in **rebirth** <term>rebirth: "Wiedergeburth," the spiritual renewal of a person through divine grace.</term>, and the **Holy Spirit** Himself, who dwells in our hearts as His temple and will tolerate no dominant sin alongside Him. c) These struggle—and indeed must struggle against one another—as long as one wishes to be a Christian. If this struggle ceases, then lust triumphs, Christianity is over, and one's status as a child of God comes to an end.

<meta>This page continues the comparison initiated on page 55, contrasting the spiritual struggle of the "reborn" Christian with the moral efforts of the "unregenerate" or pagan, specifically focusing on their differing foundations, goals, and internal discipline.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Short Reflection</header>

In the **reborn** <term>reborn: "Wiedergebohrnen," those who have undergone spiritual renewal through faith.</term>, the **Spirit** struggles against the **flesh**; among the **unregenerate**, it is **reason** against **desires**. In the reborn, it is reason **enlightened** and **strengthened** by the Spirit; among the unregenerate, it is reason left to its own devices. There is also a difference in the **foundation**. The reborn have **God’s Word and Will** as their foundation; whereas the **pagans** <note>original: "Heyden." In this context, it refers to the classical ancients and those outside the Christian faith.</note> have their **superstition**, and the particular opinions of their way of teaching and living. For just as everyone who invented a new way of teaching, or followed one invented by others, also applied themselves to living according to it. The **purpose** is also unequal. A **Christian** directs everything to **God’s honor**; the pagans seek their **own honor**. As the otherwise wise **Cicero** gives enough to understand in his speech for **Archias** <note>Referring to the speech *Pro Archia Poeta*, where Cicero discusses the natural human desire for glory and lasting fame.</note> and in a letter to **Lucceius** <note>Cicero famously wrote to the historian Lucceius asking him to chronicle Cicero's consulship in a favorable and celebratory light.</note>. Furthermore, the pagans look no further than **external works**: thoughts, they believe, are **tax-free** <note>original: "Zoll-frey." Referring to the German proverb "Gedanken sind zollfrei," meaning that thoughts are private and not subject to external judgement or "taxes."</note>; a Christian, however, must see to it that **inner purity** is attained and maintained, and that all **evil lusts** are restrained.

...many parents are damned for the sake of their children, who otherwise might well have attained salvation. Parents love their children so dearly that they cannot bring themselves to punish or discipline them. Nor should others be allowed to do so. Children, they say, should be raised "freely." Consequently, they become insolent and unruly, such that afterward they obey neither God nor man, nor are they of any use. The example of Eli <note>Eli was a biblical High Priest (1 Samuel) whose sons were corrupt. Because Eli failed to restrain them, God punished his entire family. Temporal punishment <note>original: "zeitliche Strafe," referring to physical or legal consequences suffered during one's earthly life, as opposed to eternal punishment in the afterlife.</note> followed swiftly enough upon both the father and the sons. How their souls fared, however, remains left to the Divine Judgment.

It is to be wished that all those who have children would have the story of Eli painted and hung upon every wall, and that they would contemplate it diligently every day, so that they might better use—or allow others to use—the rod <note>original: "Ruthe." Based on the proverb "spare the rod, spoil the child," the rod was viewed in this period as a necessary tool for driving out "folly" and ensuring a child’s salvation.</note> placed in their hands. Parents could have prevented this had they been more cautious and diligent in the rearing of their children. d)

### §. LXXVIII.

One should also be on guard against several Papist <note>The author uses "Papist" as a contemporary Protestant derogatory term for Roman Catholics.</note> casuists <term>casuists: theologians who study "cases of conscience," often resolving moral dilemmas through complex reasoning. As has already been shown to them by various people for a long time, one can learn evil from them sooner than good. I would advise Papist parents not to send their children to the confessional <note>original: *Beicht-Stuhl*</note>: For it seems to me they can come to no greater knowledge of sin anywhere else than in the confessionals. Because there, questions are asked about every circumstance of the sins, the youth learn much that they did not know before, and afterwards they sometimes do things they would otherwise never have done. Who does not know how lustful human nature is? Such things are like a little spark falling into tinder. This sets ablaze what would otherwise never have burned.

### §. LXXXIII.

Benefits bind one to gratitude. Whoever wishes to be grateful must flee from what the benefactor hates. Is it not then our duty to flee from these desires? Or can we deny His great benefits? He has the right to demand that we avoid evil even without a reward. Yet, according to the abundance of His immeasurable goodness, He promises many temporal as well as eternal rewards to those who hate and abandon evil. One need only read what was set down by Luther <note>Martin Luther (1483–1546); this refers to his explanation of the conclusion to the Ten Commandments in the *Small Catechism*</note> at the end of the **ten commandments** from Moses. There, **GOD** promises the righteous that He will do good to them unto the thousandth generation. Is that not a great deal? Is that not an infinitely kind **GOD**? Should one not gladly avoid all evil for His sake, even if it were ever so "useful"? The fulfillment of such glorious promises has been experienced by those who, for **GOD's** sake, would not consent to evil. Joseph, the midwives in Egypt <note>original: *Wehmütter*; referring to Shiphrah and Puah (Exodus 1), who feared God and refused to follow the Pharaoh's command to kill Hebrew infants</note>, Shadrach, Meshach, Abednego, and Daniel refused to do that which they were enticed to do. Has **GOD** [not] <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword indicates the next page begins with "nicht" (not).</note>

### §. LXXXIV.

Even if all of this did not move us, we still cannot deny our **duty** <term>Schuldigkeit; a moral or legal obligation one is bound to perform</term>. A child owes its parents, a servant his master, and a subject his **sovereign authority** <term>Obrigkeit; the governing body or person in power, such as a prince or magistrate</term> love, honor, and obedience. From whom do we have our life? From **GOD**. Thus, He is our Father. Who has snatched us out of the power of the devil? **JESUS**, the Son of **GOD**. Therefore, we must recognize **GOD** and **JESUS** as our Lord and confess that we are His servants. Who protects us in the face of such many and great dangers in which we hover? **God** alone! Thus, He does for us what a sovereign authority can do. For He is also the Supreme Lord over all lords and authorities. A Lord of all lords, a King of all kings. Should we then not owe **GOD** what children owe their parents, servants their masters, and subjects their sovereign? 1) Yes, certainly, and indeed so much more, as the greatness of our **GOD**—

### §. LXXXV.

# Brief Reflection
### of Christian Perfection.

is a spirit. What comparison can spirit have with the bodily, or what pleasure can it find in that which is entirely unlike itself? Our soul is immortal; everything earthly is mortal and transient. When one thinks to truly delight in such things, they vanish, or one must depart from them. How then can a pleasure be hoped for, or a satisfaction be attained through them? A large wine-barrel cannot be filled with a spoonful; but if one brings that same barrel to a great sea, it becomes full and more than enough remains. Everything earthly is toward our finite, yet immortal soul, like a spoonful compared to such a large barrel. Thus, the soul cannot be filled with the earthly, nor can the one be satisfied by the other. 

But **GOD** is an infinite spirit and thus an inexhaustible sea of all blessedness <term>Seligkeit; a state of supreme spiritual happiness and salvation</term>; through Him, the finite, though immortal, soul can be truly satisfied. O incomparable bliss! O unfathomable glory! O, if only one were already up there! O, give me wings! Give me wings! That we may hasten to Him who can satisfy us eternally!

## of Christian Perfection.

### §. XCII.

On the other hand, one should consider the eternal torment of Hell, where the sensualists <note>original: *Lüstlinge*; those who prioritize physical or worldly desires over spiritual ones</note>, who follow the impulse toward evil more than the Word and Will of **GOD**, run headlong. This torment is unspeakable. The Scripture presents it under the image of fire, because there is nothing in the world that causes greater pain or affects the senses more acutely than fire—yet this is not to say that there is literally physical fire in Hell. The intensity of Hell’s agony will be incomparably greater than any pain from fire. Whenever evil desire arises, one should consider the eternal life we lose if we follow that desire, and the agony of Hell we draw upon both body and soul. I believe that if such a meditation is performed earnestly and prayerfully, the desire to sin should pass away, unless a person willfully chooses to be his own traitor and murderer. In that case, he is beyond help.

# A Short Reflection

is a spirit. What comparison can he <note>Referring to God, who is described as Spirit in John 4:24</note> have with bodily things, or what pleasure can he take in that which is entirely unlike him? Our soul is immortal; all earthly things are mortal and transitory. Just when one thinks to truly delight in them, they are gone, or one must leave them behind. How then can pleasure be hoped for, or satisfaction <term>Sättigung: the state of being completely full or satisfied, often used theologically to describe the soul's rest in God</term> be attained through such things? Everything earthly, compared to our finite yet immortal soul, is like a spoonful compared to such a large cask. Just as it should happen in spirit and in truth, so must it also be continued at all times. As long as the soldier lies in the field against his enemy, he must always be ready for defense. Yet another leads to a different sort of company, where people wish to be regarded as honorable women and maidens <note>The author implies these are places of ill repute or superficial vanity hiding behind a mask of respectability</note>. On all such paths there is manifold work for such idlers <term>Müßiggänger: those who are habitually idle or lazy</term>. From this, the Devil has his profit. When reason <term>Vernunfft: the human capacity for logic and moral judgment</term> wanders in "unloving love" <note>An oxymoron suggesting a misplaced, selfish, or sinful affection</note>, then the desires are the coachman, and the lusts overturn the wagon. Thus the Devil makes his prey. In profit-seeking gambling, greed <term>Geitz: avarice or the excessive desire for wealth</term> and loveless deception do their part in the service of Hell. For the money won is a capital from which Satan collects the interest. And the money lost in gambling, just as it makes the money-bag light, so it burdens the conscience <term>Gewissen: the internal moral compass</term>. If someone said that both he who wins and he who loses is a thief, it might seem a harsh saying. But he who deprives himself of his soul, his neighbor of his money, and his wife and children of their own—what shall he be called? Or what name will the Seventh Commandment <note>original: "siebende Gebot"; in the Lutheran tradition of the author, the Seventh Commandment is "Thou shalt not steal."</note> grant him? And because in all such things, both prayer as well as devout meditation

# Of Christian Perfection.

->## §. XCVIII.<-

<margin>u) 1 Corinthians 15:33.</margin>
And what shall be said of the "refined" conversations <note>original: "saubern Gesprächen"; the author uses this ironically to describe speech that is superficially clever or witty but morally bankrupt</note> that take place during such activities of idlers <term>Müßiggänger: those who are habitually idle or avoid spiritual duties in favor of worldly distractions</term>? They are of such a nature that evil desires are more promoted than prevented by them. "Bad company ruins good morals." <note>original: "Böse Geschwäße verderben gute Sitten"; a translation of the Greek proverb famously quoted by the Apostle Paul in 1 Corinthians 15:33</note> u) Jest and foolish chatter <term>Narrentheidung: a term for senseless, empty, or worldly gossip</term>, filthy jokes—even if presented in a sharp and elegant manner—support cravings in every way and imprint much sinful imagery upon the imagination <term>Einbildungs-Krafft: literally "the power of imprinting images"; the psychological faculty that pietist authors believed could be corrupted by external sensory input</term>. What else can follow from this, but that at the first opportunity, these desires break out through deeds and reveal themselves? Anyone who has experienced such things and examines them impartially must confess that he is much less capable of resisting desires the more he indulges in such matters. Oh, if only one were wise and remained idle from all such "busy idleness"! He who truly loves his soul does this; he who does not do so hates himself and his own salvation <term>Seligkeit: the state of eternal blessedness or being saved</term>.

### §. XCIX.

<margin>x) Hosea 4:11.</margin>
If desire is not to triumph, men must not allow themselves to be defeated by drink. Can a dead man defend himself, or one who is bound resist the enemy? Just as little strength has a full stomach <note>original: "voller Kropff"; literally a "full crop," a derogatory term comparing a glutton's gullet to that of a bird</note> against evil lusts. Oh, what a convenient means gluttony <term>Völlerey: excessive eating and drinking; intemperance</term> is for the devil to plunge people into all sorts of sins! "Wine, new wine, and harlotry make one mad." <note>original: "Wein, Most und Hurerey machen toll"; a translation of the Latin Vulgate for Hosea 4:11: *vinum et ebrietas et fornicatio auferunt cor*</note> x) Beer and brandy <term>Brandtwein: distilled spirits or liquor</term> can do it too. What good can be expected from mad people? When the blood is heated, the desires burn. Then one rages, fumes, and storms. Then one wishes to perform "heroic deeds," even though neither hand nor foot can do their part. 

Thus Noah gave offense to his children—he who otherwise has the reputation in the Scriptures for great piety. <note>Referencing Genesis 9, where Noah became drunk from his vineyard and lay uncovered in his tent, causing shame to his sons.</note> Alexander <note>Alexander the Great (356–323 BCE), often used in moral literature as an example of a conqueror who could rule the world but not his own temper</note> overcame all enemies; but when he was drunk, he could not overcome himself. He murdered his best friends and afterwards, as punishment, wanted to kill himself. Are these not the terrible effects of drunkenness? Another is ignited by lewd passion, so that his heart seethes, as did Holofernes toward... <note>Referencing the Book of Judith, where the Assyrian general Holofernes’s drunken infatuation led to his decapitation.</note>

### §. C. [100]

It will also be a good means of resisting lusts and dampening desires if one does not love the world and oneself excessively. If we love the world, then we want to make ourselves equal to her as our friend, not lose her friendship, and not be contrary to her. We also do not want to be contrary to our own flesh and blood, as a piece of the world. Then lust easily triumphs, and evil is accomplished before one would have even expected it. Therefore, God has not warned in His Word in vain: "Do not conform yourselves to this world." <note>Romans 12:2</note> "Do not love the world, nor what is in the world," carnal— <note>original: "Fleisch-"; the text breaks here and continues on the next page with the suffix "-liches," forming "Fleischliches" or carnal/fleshly things.</note>

### §. CIII. [103]

<margin>f) Matthew 15:9. Isaiah 29:13.</margin>
One's own devotion and self-chosen works <note>original: "selbst-erwehlte Wercke"; religious practices invented by humans rather than commanded by God.</note> are also not sufficient. **GOD** does not want to be served and honored according to our will, but according to His. Therefore He says:

> In vain do they serve me, while they teach such doctrines that are nothing but the commandments of men. f)

Are not all self-chosen works clearly enough rejected here? What, then, will those have to hope for who want to serve **GOD** not according to **GOD's** will, but according to their own devotion? Is it not so? If you command your servant to do something, must he not do as you have said? If he had done something else, or perhaps even done it to please others, but then came afterward and wanted a reward from you, what would you say or give to him? I suspect neither would be very pleasant.

### §. 105.

Beyond this, there is another dangerous path upon which many a wanderer runs toward destruction. These are the customs and statutes <note>original: "Gewohnheiten und Satzungen"; refers to man-made traditions or legalistic rules.</note> that are invented by men and accepted by men. These are regarded by **GOD** no differently than those mentioned before, and they deserve not grace, but wrath in every respect.

### §. 106.

"Yes, let the Papists <note>A term, often used pejoratively by Protestants during this era, to refer to members of the Roman Catholic Church.</note> look to that," (so many will say), "for we ground our teaching upon **GOD’s** Word." I answer: The Papists must certainly do the same. But what does it profit us that our doctrine is grounded in **GOD’s** Word if our life runs contrary to it? If we look at our conduct <note>original: "Wandel"; a term for one's overall lifestyle and moral behavior.</note>, we will find and must confess—if we do not wish to deceive ourselves—that our life is, in many things, more in accordance with worldly custom—or as it is now called, the "fashion" <note>original: "Mode"; the author is critiquing the tendency to follow social trends rather than biblical principles.</note>—than with the Word of **GOD**.

Even though a worldly nature <note>original: "Weltenhende Wesen"; likely referring to an attachment or conformity to the world.</note> is clearly enough forbidden to us. As Paul speaks: "Do not conform yourselves..." <note>A reference to Romans 12:2: "And do not be conformed to this world, but be transformed by the renewing of your mind."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on whether it is permissible to follow a fashion once it has become established. It specifically addresses the moral distinction between fashions that are merely vain and those that are inherently immodest.</meta>

<page-num>94</page-num>
<header>A Brief Consideration</header>

...taken, may I not then follow?
Not without distinction. If the defect <note>original: "Mangel"; here meaning a moral flaw, a failing in propriety, or a shortcoming in the garment's purpose.</note> was initially only with the people who, out of curiosity, pride <note>original: "Hoffarth"; a strong 17th-century term for haughty vanity or worldly arrogance.</note> and so forth, introduced it, but not in the garment itself—as the clothing contained nothing evil in its own nature—then one may well use it once it has become common everywhere. However, if the defect is in the garment itself, then one can never adopt such a fashion. Now, the defect is in the garment when its purpose <note>The "purpose" of clothing in this theological context is to provide modest covering as mandated after the Fall of Man.</note> is not achieved, in that those parts of the body remain bare which might be offensive to one's neighbor and could awaken sinful thoughts. This includes when women <note>original: "Frauenzimmer"; a collective term for women, often used in a formal or moralizing tone in this period.</note> expose themselves so much at the neck and breasts. Whatever thoughts they themselves have about it, and whatever reasons they may have, remains a matter for **God** alone. He examines the heart and the kidneys <note>original: "Herzen und Nieren"; a traditional biblical expression (derived from Psalm 7:9 and Jeremiah 17:10) meaning that God searches a person’s innermost thoughts, emotions, and secret motives.</note>. Before Him, no denial nor vain excuse will find any room. But even though their thoughts may be as pure internally as they wish, it still never happens externally without offense <note>original: "Aergerniß"; in this religious context, it refers to a "stumbling block" or a "scandal" that causes another person to fall into sin.</note>. And thus it is punishable before **God**, even if only a single person should be offended by it. If one wants to say: "Indeed, why should I [concern myself] with another's...

Furthermore, it is also a defect in clothing when it is designed too luxuriously and deceitfully <note>original: "gauckelhafftig"; meaning frivolous, showy, or intended to create a false impression, like a performer's costume.</note>. In particular, it is a great and harmful failing when one hangs far too much upon the body, acquiring everything at too high a cost and too expensively. Through this, many a person makes himself a beggar, or perhaps even a thief, by taking more from the merchant <note>original: "Kramer"; a shopkeeper or cloth-seller.</note> than he is able to pay. Many give little thought to how their children should be properly raised and their families properly provided for, so long as they themselves can always walk about magnificently. Yet Paul says: n)

### §. 117.

Among Christians, one indeed has **God’s** Word. Yet there are many who have no certainty regarding their actions: instead, they often doubt whether this or that aligns with **God’s** Word, and thus also, whether it should be done or left undone. In such a case, it is necessary to stand still <note>The phrase "stille stehe" suggests a spiritual pause or quiet waiting to gain clarity before acting.</note> and strive to attain a complete certainty; or, where that cannot be had, one should not perform the work. But he who continues while he still stands in doubt commits a sin, even if the work he does were in itself in accordance with the Word of **God** and therefore good. This is because his work, as Paul teaches, does not proceed from **faith**. s) Furthermore, such a person makes it clear enough that he is not so much concerned with **God’s** Word and will as he is with his own will, which he intends to fulfill whether it aligns with **God’s** will or not.

and cannot demand the same thing from everyone, a person must make a clear distinction, so that he may know what is fitting for him and what may be required of him in the <term>final accounting<gloss>original: "Rechnung." The theological concept of the Day of Judgment where every individual must give an account of their life to God.</gloss></term>. Referring to the "Three Estates" model of society: the church, the state, and the household/labor.</gloss></term>, noble or common, authority or subject, master or servant; and at other circumstances, whether one is rich or poor, high or low, learned or unlearned, and so forth. In this regard, everyone must learn their duty and the works that can be required of them from **God’s** Word and from those books which treat of duties. Those who cannot do this for themselves must be taught by their superiors. Simple people will be able to learn as much as is necessary for them from the Catechism and the <term>Table of Duties<gloss>original: "Haus-Tafel." # Of Christian Perfection.

There is always worry, fear, hope, and finally death, both for the one who sits in high honors and for the lowliest on earth. Both for the one who wears silk and a crown, and for the one who wears a <term>coarse smock<gloss>original: "groben Kittel." A simple, cheap garment worn by laborers or the poor.</gloss></term>. There is always anger, <term>zeal<gloss>original: "Eifer." In this context, it refers to a restless or jealous passion rather than positive enthusiasm.</gloss></term>, adversity, discord, and danger of death, envy, and quarreling. And these things affect Christians all the more often, the more the devil and the world hate them.

But the most bitter, most painful, and most violently piercing thing is the inner <term>suffering of the soul<gloss>original: "Seelen-Leiden." This refers to psychological and spiritual distress, often characterized in this era as "trials of the soul."</gloss></term>. In this state, a person is tormented in many ways. There, Satan pushes all kinds of evil and shameful thoughts into the person's mind. Even during the holiest and purest activities, he often creates the most hideous and ugly mental images. At this, a pious heart is terrified, feeling trembling, anxiety, and dread. 

Often he incites a person toward this or that sin with such intensity that one must wonder, with trembling and horror, how one could even have come upon such thoughts. At other times, he knows how to magnify the sins that a person committed—either knowingly before <term>conversion<gloss>original: "Bekehrung." The turning point where a person commits their life to faith.</gloss></term>, or after conversion out of weakness, ignorance, or haste—making them appear so large that out of fear

### § 123.

<margin>g) Psalm 73.</margin>
Alongside this <term>spiritual suffering<gloss>original: "geistliches Leiden." In this context, it refers to the internal "trials of the soul" or "Anfechtungen" discussed on the previous page, such as doubt or feeling forsaken by God.</gloss></term>, there is also physical suffering. This occurs either within our own body, such as illness and various infirmities, or outside the body, affecting our honor, property, spouses, children, friends, and relatives. All of this, however, is only like a shadow compared to the body when measured against the spiritual suffering mentioned before. All misfortune is powerful against a person only in proportion to how strong or weak <term>self-will<gloss>original: "Eigenwille." A central concept in Pietist devotion referring to the human ego's desires that act in opposition to God's will.</gloss></term> and the love of earthly things are within that person. Where there is no self-will and no excessive love for the earthly, no external misfortune can cause particular torment. For it is not the misfortune itself, but rather our own self-will and unbridled love for the world that cause us distress. Whoever can deny themselves and surrender their will solely to God's will shall never consider themselves wretched; nor will they regard anything as a loss, unless they were to lose the grace of God. Such a person says with David: 
> If I only have you, O God, then I ask for nothing in heaven or on earth. g)

Brief Meditation 112

one day or another the <term>post-horn is blown<gloss>original: "zur Poſt geblaſen." This refers to the signal given by a postillion or mail carrier, signifying that it is time for the traveler to depart and move on to the next stage of the journey.</gloss></term>, he does not let any of this hold him back, nor does he grieve that he must leave it: but rather, when he has paid his dues and thanked the host, he departs with as much good courage as he enjoyed the comfort. If he comes to a bad inn, where the people are rude, the hospitality poor, and the room full of stench and filth, then he thinks: "Ah, it looks vexing here." But the best thing is that it does not last long. Departure ends the hardship. And so he remains in this one with as much good courage as in the other. He leaves both with a contented heart—and indeed, the latter even more gladly, because it only wanted to cause him annoyance. If we can and will view everything in this great <term>World-Inn<gloss>original: "Welt-Herberge." A common spiritual metaphor in this period representing the earthly world as a temporary, often uncomfortable lodging where the soul stays only briefly before reaching its true home in heaven.</gloss></term> in this way, then it will not be hard for us to endure evil and to lose the good.

# [Of] Christian Perfection.

the heart on earthly things, they will not be lost without **heartache** <note>original: "Herzeleid." This implies deep emotional sorrow or "heart-sorrow."</note>. What is loosely glued is easily torn apart again without noise; but what is firmly glued is not separated again without a loud cracking. So it is with everything in the world: the more one loves a thing, the more one is distressed when one cannot have it or keep it. Then one weeps, sighs, laments, howls, and strikes oneself in misery beyond measure. However, the less one takes earthly things into the heart, the less one feels their lack and loss. This maintains the **peace of the soul** <term>peace of the soul<gloss>original: "Ruhe des Gemüthes." In 17th-century spirituality, "Gemüth" refers to the seat of the emotions, will, and intellect—the inner disposition.</gloss></term>, while the other reveals the unruliness of the heart.

->§. CXXVIII.<-

How patiently **JESUS** endured the most intense pains for us mankind. He did not open His mouth, like a lamb that is led to the slaughtering block. k) He bore pains that He did not deserve. He bore pains for the sake of the sins of others. Should we then, in the suffering which we all have deserved more than enough, not be patient and put a bridle on the unruly flesh? <note>original: "Fleische." <header>Brief Meditation</header>

...tiently and willingly follow the suffering **JESUS**. <note>The text continues from the previous page: "patiently [gedul-dig] and willingly..."</note>
Other true lovers of piety have done the same. Consider the three men who confidently entered the fiery furnace <note>A reference to Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego from the Book of Daniel, chapter 3, who were protected by God after refusing to worship a golden image.</note>, and Daniel, who without any impatience allowed himself to be cast into the lions' den <note>Daniel 6.</note>, and all of them together who let themselves be thrown into the gaping jaws of death. Was that not terrifying? Or is anything more terrifying than a death looming before one’s eyes? Yet one did not hear them wailing or impatiently murmuring against **GOD**.

How courageously did the Maccabean martyrs <note>A reference to the accounts in 2 Maccabees, specifically the seven brothers and their mother who endured horrific torture and death rather than violate God's law.</note> endure all their pains! How courageously, confidently, and undauntedly did the Christians in the early Church—and even in more recent times—face martyrdom and death? What **God** did through them, He can also do in us and others. If we only allow faith to produce a similar love within us, and do not hinder the working of that love through the tenderness of our flesh <note>original: "Fleisches Zärtlichkeit." In this context, "tenderness" refers to a physical or moral weakness—the human tendency to shrink away from discomfort or pain.</note>. Where faith is right and love is right, no misfortune is so great that it should turn us against **God** or make us impatient.

<header>of Christian Perfection.</header>
H 2

**Works**, in which faith proves itself active through love; to this, everything required in Christianity can be traced. Where such a faith exists, there is a perfect means to arrive at perfect glory and happiness <note>original: "Glückseligkeit." In this Pietist context, the word implies both a state of earthly peace and the ultimate "blessedness" of salvation in heaven.</note>. This is according to the measure prescribed for you in this life, and as far as such things can be hoped for and attained. To be sure, one does not find an equal number of works in everyone or at all times. But this takes nothing away from faith. After all, a tree does not always have an equal amount of fruit. And yet it is a perfect and fruitful tree. It is enough that it has the power to bear fruit, and shows fruit when it is the time to bear. Thus, times can also be found where faith has no works. In the meantime, however, it is always ready to act. And when the time and opportunity to act arise, it does not fail to do so, according as one’s calling, office, station, age, gender, and so forth require or allow <note>The author refers to the concept of "vocation"—the idea that God is served through one's specific role in society, whether as a parent, worker, or citizen.</note>.

### §. CXXXI.

If we now have a perfect means, and through it can also attain the goal—which is a perfect happiness—

### §. CXXXII.

That the best and most useful treasure resides in this Kingdom, and that the greatest and most perfect happiness of this life consists in attaining it, is evident from what Christ says: *Seek first the kingdom of God and his righteousness.* 1) That which we should seek first and most must surely not be anything minor, but rather the best, most useful, and most necessary thing. If something better were to be attained in this life, Jesus would not have concealed it from us or established this [as the priority]. Furthermore, it follows that one—

<header>Brief Reflection</header>

## §. CXXXIV.

Furthermore, eternal salvation <note>original: "Seligkeit"</note> cannot be attained unless we are already in the Kingdom of God here, and possess the Kingdom of God within us. To be sure, we readily admit that there is a great difference between the happiness <note>original: "Glückseligkeit"</note> of this life and eternal salvation; yet it cannot be denied that both are firmly linked together, so that they cannot be separated. He who possesses the Kingdom of God here can, provided he perseveres in it, become an heir of Heaven. And whoever wishes to have a part in eternal life must possess the Kingdom of God here. For both are acquired through the same means, both are offered through the same means, and both are accepted through the same means. From all of this it is as clear as day <note>original: "Sonnenklar," literally "clear as the sun"</note> that nothing is more noble, nothing more excellent, nothing more glorious than the Kingdom of God, and thus nothing more blessed than to possess it. Thus we can conceive of no other purpose in our **Christianity** than the Kingdom of God. Even the **Mystics** <note>referring to the "Mystischen," a tradition of religious thought emphasizing the direct, inward experience of God's presence</note> will not be able to show us anything better.

## §. CXXXV.

But let us ask, where is this to be found—namely, in a more precise sense?

->## §. CXXXVI.<-

Thus, the monks will certainly be in the wrong if they imagine that perfection—and thus also the Kingdom of God, in which it consists—is found with them; and that the monastic state <note>original: "Münch-Stand"</note> is a "state of perfection." For if that were true, one could indeed say: "Here it is," or "there it is." Jesus says it is in us. Monasticism <note>original: "Müncherey," a term often used by critics of the period to describe the system of monastic life</note>, however, is something external, divided into many different orders <note>referring to religious orders like the Benedictines or Franciscans</note> that even frequently struggle against one another. Yet the Kingdom of God is at unity with itself and is always the same.

—would likely be the claim of those who boast so much of an **inward Christ**, an **internal Word**, an **inner natural light**, a **spiritual light**, and a **seed** <note>These terms were commonly used by Radical Pietists and Quakers to describe a spark of divinity they believed existed within every human being regardless of formal religious training.</note>. If that were true, there would have to be something inherently good in us by nature. For who would consider **GOD** and **Jesus** to be something evil? If they were part of our nature, we would also have to be in a state of **Grace** <term>Gnade: the unearned favor and love of God</term> by nature. For **GOD** and **Jesus** will not be angry with themselves, nor with that which is a part of them. But **Scripture** will have none of this. It says that we are by nature **children of wrath** <note>original: "Kinder des Zorns"; a reference to the doctrine of Original Sin, suggesting humans are born separated from God's favor.</note>. q) Thus, by nature, nothing good can be in us. Therefore, it is manifest that we cannot hope for or possess this from within ourselves; rather, it must come into us from the outside. If it were already in us, why would **Christ** teach us to pray: "Thy Kingdom come"?

One should only believe that **Jesus** is in us, and we are in him. One must also live in such a way that this noble guest is not chased away by sins. Then our happiness is as great as it can possibly be in this life. Where **Jesus** is, there is life and full sufficiency. a) Where **GOD** is, there is heaven, and eternal salvation follows, which we can hope for as certainly as if we already possessed it. Therefore *Paul* says that we are saved in hope. b) If only we remain constant in faith. For he who perseveres to the end will be (eternally) saved. c) What more do we want? Or what could we wish for that is better? Can there truly be a greater happiness than having **GOD** and **JESUS**? *David* knows of none. Therefore he says: If I only have you, O **God**, I ask for nothing in heaven or on earth. d)

Of external peace, it is said that it is the best of all things. This is certainly true regarding external matters. ->§. 151.<-

The pagan sages <note>the non-Christian philosophers of antiquity</note> also well understood that peace of mind and a good conscience are a glorious treasure. But they did not know the right means to obtain them, nor the foundation upon which they stand. What Seneca, Epictetus, and the Stoics <note>Seneca and Epictetus were prominent philosophers of Stoicism, a school that taught that virtue and self-control lead to emotional stability, or "ataraxia."</note> put forward will not stand the test <note>original: *will den Stich nicht halten*; a German idiom meaning something will not hold up under scrutiny or pressure.</note>.

->§. 152.<-

And again he says that there is no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus <note>a reference to Romans 8:1.</note>.

### § 154.

If this benefit is glorious, then the other is no less to be highly valued—namely, the **joy**, which is also cited here as a fruit of the **Holy Spirit** dwelling in the heart. Joy is a movement of our heart and mind, which arises because of the good that is present in deed, in thought, or in hope. If, then, the most **Holy Trinity** dwelling in our heart is an undeniably incomparable good; and if the benefits that **Jesus** earned for us, which we possess in **Christ**, are excellent treasures; then I believe we have reason enough to rejoice. ### §. CLVIII.

This indeed represents a heroic courage, a spiritual defiance <note>The original German "Troß" likely refers to "Trotz," meaning defiance or boldness in the face of adversity.</note>, and a holy joy. In this state, the believer steps forward to meet all enemies—both spiritual and physical—with bravery, comfort, and joy. He defies all misfortune for the sole reason that he has **GOD** and **JESUS** present with him, and possesses in Him righteousness, and thus also the peace that is bound to it. From this source springs such joy and cheerfulness. And where these are found, other genuine Christians can likewise rejoice and be happy even in the midst of the greatest misfortune.

### §. CLIX.

"Yet words are but words," says the mocker. "When it comes down to the matter itself, they might feel quite differently." Yes, yes! Mockers will be mockers. They do not understand the power of the Spirit which drives the godly; nor do they understand the impulse of love that compels them to risk and endure everything for the sake of **JESUS**. They would have no reason to mock if they would only read **Hebrews 11**. <note>Hebrews 11 is often called the "Hall of Faith," listing biblical figures who endured extreme suffering through their trust in God.</note> There we are presented with miserable, troubled—

<summary>This page describes the "heroic courage" and holy joy that arise from a believer's union with Christ, which provides peace even in the face of physical or spiritual enemies. It further defends this spiritual strength against skeptics by pointing to the historical examples of endurance found in Hebrews 11.</summary>

<keywords>heroic courage, spiritual boldness, holy joy, righteousness, peace, mockers, power of the spirit, Hebrews 11, Romans 8</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 141, completing the description of the faithful figures mentioned in Hebrews 11.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>
<header>Short Reflection</header>

—persons described, who however did not let themselves be led astray in their piety by their cross <note>In this context, "cross" refers to the specific trials or sufferings an individual must endure as part of their faith.</note>, nor did they allow themselves to fall away from **GOD**. Anyone who has read **2 Corinthians 11 and 12** will not be able to suspect **Paul** of having words without works. Indeed! He has proven in deed what he expressed in words. The examples of so many hundreds, or rather thousands of martyrs are well known from the history books. These went toward death not only willingly, but with joy. Now, death is the most terrible of all terrible things. <note>This echoes the Aristotelian sentiment that death is the "most fearful of all things," making the martyrs' joy a supernatural feat.</note> For one who despises death, whom shall he fear? For one who meets it with joy, what shall grieve him?

### §. CLX.

No one would have possessed such courage if they had not been righteous in faith and united with **God**. It is only **God** the **Holy Spirit** who works such peace, such joy, such courage, and such steadfastness. To be sure, the heathens <note>Referring here to the ancient Greeks, Romans, or other non-Christian peoples of antiquity.</note> have also displayed courage. Like **Agag** the **Amalekite**, who went confidently to **Samuel** and said: "Surely the bitterness of death must be driven away in this manner." <margin>x) 1 Samuel 15:32 <note>The author uses a slightly different phrasing than modern English Bibles, which usually read "Surely the bitterness of death is past."</note></margin> But it is only a facade and a
blind— <meta>catchword: Blend-</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the joy and courage shown by martyrs in the face of death—the most terrifying of things—proves the genuine power of the Holy Spirit over mere words. It contrasts this spiritual steadfastness with the superficial or "blind" courage of the heathens, such as the biblical Agag.</summary>

—deception. They did not suffer death for the sake of truth and righteousness. ### §. CLXII.

This peace and this joy is the true foretaste of eternal life, which **GOD** grants to one in a lesser measure and to another in a greater measure. Beyond this, we should desire nothing more. Whoever desires more from **GOD** than what has been promised raises himself above Him and wishes to prescribe laws to Him regarding how He should behave toward mankind and how He should distribute His gifts of grace among them. This must necessarily displease **GOD** and result in punishment rather than grace. Granted, we cannot bind **GOD’s** hands so that He should do no more than what is promised in the **Scripture**. For we see that He also occasionally allows something extraordinary to happen to His beloved ones, as occurred with certain disciples on Mount **Tabor** <note>original: "Berge Thabor." This refers to the Transfiguration of Jesus (Matthew 17), where Peter, James, and John were given a glimpse of Christ's divine glory.</note>

<meta>catchword: -bor,</meta>

<summary>The author explains that the internal peace and joy felt by the righteous are proofs of God's grace and a foretaste of heaven. He warns readers not to demand more spiritual signs than God has promised, while acknowledging that God sometimes grants extraordinary experiences like the Transfiguration.</summary>

Thus, this peace and joy are such an excellent benefit that we can well be satisfied with them. Whoever attains more, or even attains this in a higher degree than usual, let him thank **GOD**. But whoever demands more travels upon Enthusiastic <term>Enthusiastic (Enthusiastisch): In this 18th-century context, "Enthusiasm" or "fanaticism" referred to radical religious movements that prioritized personal visions and direct "inner light" over the established word of the Bible.</term> paths.

And what, after all, should it be that **GOD** would give us further, beyond what we can have according to the content of His Word? Or what can all revelations, ecstasies, <note>original: "Entzückungen."</note> or whatever else they may be called, show us that is better than what is presented in the Word? Or is there anything better than **GOD**, who has united Himself with us? With what could someone be satisfied if they are not satisfied with **GOD**? Or is anything more useful and glorious than the peace and joy that the **Holy Spirit** wishes to work in those who are justified?

### §. CLXIII.

And for this very reason—because it is pure grace—we should not imagine that we must always feel and sense this testimony of the Holy Spirit, which He sends into us through peace and joy. Oh no! The Bridegroom of the Soul <note>original: "Seelen-Bräutigam," a common Pietist metaphor for Christ's intimate relationship with the believer's soul.</note> sometimes stands behind the wall and hides His face for a little while, <margin>Song of Solomon 2:9; Isaiah 54:8</margin> in that He allows even the most pious to fall into great spiritual temptation <term>Anfechtung: A term describing intense spiritual trials, doubt, or a sense of being abandoned by God, intended to refine the believer's faith.</term> or permits them to be burdened with a great cross.

### §. 165.

People will sense such a taste of God’s friendliness all the more, the fewer sins are found within their conscience. For although **GOD** forgives all sins to the penitent <note>Those who have repented and turned away from their sins.</note> and does not count against them what happens afterward out of ignorance, haste, and weakness, these failings are still harmful and vexing to a person insofar as they hinder the Holy Spirit from providing His testimony so perceptibly. He desires a quiet soul and a conscience that is conscious of no sin. And this is precisely the rest, the leisure, the

For there is no sin so small that it could not set the soul in unrest and stir up the conscience. Even a slight wind can disturb the sea so that it casts up waves. In the same way, small sins, because they provoke unrest in the devout, prevent the sensation of peace in the conscience. Therefore, one has enough reason to flee even small sins, so that peace and joy—or the testimony of the Holy Spirit—is not hindered in our soul.

### §. CLXVIII.

Therefore, one should not be amazed when the mystics complain that, although they strive most intensely for it, they do not attain their goal, nor do they arrive at true spiritual experience. Most of them are <term>teachers of works</term> <note>German: *Werck-Lehrer*. This refers to people who believe that spiritual standing or salvation is earned through human effort and outward deeds rather than through divine grace.</note>, who are not only not free from small sins, but still have the full weight of all their sins hanging upon their necks: because on the path where they strut <note>The original *pausend* suggests walking with pride, swelling with self-importance, or "puffing oneself up" with one's own merit.</note> in their own righteousness, they find no forgiveness of sins at all. For such forgiveness is found only in the wounds of **Jesus**, and indeed only by those who seek righteousness through faith in Christ alone. The more and the more wisely we strive to be without sin, the clearer and more powerful our experience will be, and the more distinctly and palpably will the <term>Holy Spirit</term> <note>Original: *H. Geist* (Heiliger Geist).</note> provide His testimony within us through peace and joy.
